s_{1}

“promise”
each other that the target state might still be reachable with probability 1, but these promises depend on each other cyclically.
Removing the internal behaviour of this EC and
“merging”

\hat{s}
and
s_{1}
into a single state (with only action
b_{1}
) solves this issue.

In general, by definition of ECs, every state inside an EC can be reached from any other state with probability 1.
Since we are interested in (unbounded) reachability, this means that for an EC there can only be two cases.
Either, the EC contains a target state.
Then, reaching any state of the EC is (a.s.) equivalent to reaching the target already and we do not need to treat the internal transitions of the EC further.
Otherwise, i.e. when the EC does not contain a target state, we can also omit treatment of its internal behaviour and only consider its interaction with outside states.
For the remainder of the section, fix an arbitrary MDP
\mathcal{M}=(S,Act,{Av},\Delta)
, starting state
\hat{s}
, target set
T
, and precision
\varepsilon>0
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
(R,B)\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M})
be an EC of
\mathcal{M}
.
Then,
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\max}[\Diamond\{s^{\prime}\}]=1
for any states
s,s^{\prime}\in R
and consequently
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\max}[\Diamond T]=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s^{\prime}}^{\max}[\Diamond T]
for any target set
T\subseteq S
.

Proof 4.3
.

Follows directly from
[
52
, Lemma 1]
(observe that the first claim is a special case of the second claim with
T=\{s^{\prime}\}
).

In other words, states in the same EC are equivalent for reachability and we can apply a quotienting construction w.r.t. to ECs.
This idea has been exploited by the
MEC quotient
construction
[
61
,
52
,
73
]
, a preprocessing step where first all MECs are identified and then
“collapsed”
into a representative state.
However, this approach requires that the whole graph structure of the MDP is known.
Constructing the whole graph of the system may be prohibitively expensive or even impossible, as, e.g., in our limited knowledge setting (see
Definition

5.2
).
Hence, we propose a modification to the BRTDP algorithm, which detects and handles ECs
“on-the-fly”
.
The algorithm will repeatedly identify ECs and maintain a separate, simplified MDP, which is similar to a MEC quotient.

4.1
Collapsing End Components

As already explained, collapsing an EC can be viewed as replacing it with a single representative state, omitting the internal behaviour of the EC.
In the following definition, we introduce the
collapsed MDP
, where end components are merged into representative states.
Moreover, we again introduce the special states
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
, acting as a target and sink respectively, to avoid corner cases.
Many statements in this section are similar to
[
61
, Section 6.4]
but adapted to our particular use case.
Note that our definition of collapsed MDP in particular depends on the target set
T
.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\mathsf{EC}=\{(R_{1},B_{1}),\dots,(R_{n},B_{n})\}\subseteq\EcsOp(\mathcal{M})
be a (possibly empty) set of ECs in
\mathcal{M}
with
R_{i},B_{i}\neq\emptyset
and pairwise disjoint.
Define
R_{\mathsf{EC}}=\bigcup_{i}R_{i}
and
B_{\mathsf{EC}}=\bigcup_{i}B_{i}
the set of all states and actions in
\mathsf{EC}
, respectively.

The
collapsed MDP
is defined as
\mathcal{M}^{c}=(S^{c},Act^{c},{Av}^{c},\Delta^{c})=\collapse(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{EC},\hat{s},T)
,

•

S^{c}=S\setminus R_{\mathsf{EC}}\mathbin{\cup}\{s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}\}\mathbin{\cup}\{s_{+},s_{-}\}
, where
s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}\notin S
are new
representative
states,
s_{+}
is the new target state, and
s_{-}
is a new sink state,

•

Act^{c}=Act\setminus B_{\mathsf{EC}}\mathbin{\cup}\{\mathrm{rem}_{i}\}\mathbin{\cup}\{a_{+},a_{-}\}
, where
\mathrm{rem}_{i}\notin Act
are new
remain
actions (one per state, as we assume actions to be uniquely associated with one state),

•

{Av}^{c}(s)
is defined by

–

{Av}^{c}(s)={Av}(s)
for
s\in S\setminus R_{\mathsf{EC}}
,
8
8

8

Recall that actions in
B_{\mathsf{EC}}
are only available for states in
R_{\mathsf{EC}}
, hence
{Av}(s)\subseteq Act^{c}
for other states.

–

{Av}^{c}(s_{(R_{i},B_{i})})=\bigcup_{s\in R_{i}}{Av}(s)\setminus B_{i}\mathbin{\cup}\{\mathrm{rem}_{i}\}
,

–

{Av}^{c}(s_{+})=\{a_{+}\}
,
{Av}^{\prime}(s_{-})=\{a_{-}\}
, and

•

\Delta^{c}
is defined by (
\mathsf{states}
is an auxiliary function defined below)

–

\Delta^{c}(s^{c},a^{c},s^{\prime c})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{states}(s^{\prime c})}\Delta(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M}),a^{c},s^{\prime})
for
s^{c},s^{\prime c}\in S^{c}\setminus\{s_{+},s_{-}\}
and
a^{c}\in{Av}^{c}(s^{c})\mathbin{\cap}B
,

–

\Delta^{c}(s_{(R_{i},B_{i})},\mathrm{rem}_{i})=\{s_{+}\mapsto 1\}
if
T\mathbin{\cap}R_{i}\neq\emptyset
and
\{s_{-}\mapsto 1\}
otherwise, and

–

\Delta^{c}(s_{+},a_{+},s_{+})=1
,
\Delta^{\prime}(s_{-},a_{-},s_{-})=1
,

with the following auxiliary functions

•

\mathsf{collapsed}:S\to S^{c}
maps states of
\mathcal{M}
to their corresponding state in the collapsed MDP, i.e.
\mathsf{collapsed}(s)=s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
if
s\in R_{i}
for some
i
and
\mathsf{collapsed}(s)=s
otherwise,

•

\mathsf{states}:S^{c}\setminus\{s_{+},s_{-}\}\to 2^{S}
maps states in the collapsed MDP to the set of states they represent, i.e.
\mathsf{states}(s^{c})=R_{i}
if
s^{c}=s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
for some
i
and
\mathsf{states}(s^{c})=\{s^{c}\}\subseteq S
otherwise,

•

\mathsf{equiv}:S\to 2^{S}
maps states of
\mathcal{M}
to all states in their EC, i.e.
\mathsf{equiv}(s)=R_{i}
if
s\in R_{i}
for some
i
and
\mathsf{equiv}(s)=\{s\}
otherwise.

Note that
\mathsf{equiv}(s)=\mathsf{states}(\mathsf{collapsed}(s))
.
For ease of notation, we extend these auxiliary functions to sets of states in the obvious way, i.e.
\mathsf{collapsed}(R)=\{\mathsf{collapsed}(s)\mid s\in R\}
,
\mathsf{states}(R^{c})=\bigcup_{s^{c}\in R^{c}}\mathsf{states}(s^{c})
, and
\mathsf{equiv}(R)=\bigcup_{s\in R}\mathsf{equiv}(s)
.
Finally, if
\hat{s}\in R_{i}
for some
i
, we identify
\hat{s}
with
s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
for ease of notation.
This guarantees that we always have
\hat{s}\in S^{c}
.

Figure 4:
Example of an MDP (left) and its collapsed version (right) with
T=\{s_{2}\}
and
\mathsf{EC}=\{(\{\hat{s},s_{1}\},\{a_{0},a_{1}\}),(\{s_{2},s_{3}\},\{a_{2},a_{3}\})\}
.

See
Figure

4
for an example of a collapsed MDP.
Observe that given a set
\mathsf{EC}
explicitly, the collapsed MDP can be computed on-the-fly, i.e. without constructing the original MDP completely.
In particular, for a state
s
in the MDP
\mathcal{M}
, we can compute the corresponding state
s^{c}=\mathsf{collapsed}(s)
as well as
{Av}^{c}(s^{c})
and
\Delta^{c}(s^{c},a^{c})
for all actions
a\in{Av}^{c}(s^{c})
, based on the given set
\mathsf{EC}
.

Now, we prove some useful properties about the collapsed MDP.
These properties are rather intuitive, however the corresponding proofs are surprisingly technical without revealing relevant insights.
Thus, the proofs may be skipped.
In essence, we prove that (i) there is a correspondence of paths between the original and the collapsed MDP, (ii) there is a correspondence of ECs between the two MDPs, and, most importantly, (iii) the reachability probability is equal on the two MDPs.

Fix a collapsed MDP of
\mathcal{M}
as
\mathcal{M}^{c}=(S^{c},Act^{c},{Av}^{c},\Delta^{c})=\collapse(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{EC},\hat{s},T)
for the remainder of this section, where
\mathsf{EC}=\{(R_{i},B_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
is any appropriate set of end components.

Lemma 4.5
.

We have that
\mathsf{collapsed}(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}))=\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}^{c})
for all
a\in Act\mathbin{\cap}Act^{c}
.

Proof 4.6
.

First, observe that
Act\mathbin{\cap}Act^{c}=Act^{c}\setminus\{a_{+},a_{-},\mathrm{rem}_{i}\}
by definition.
The claim follows by a case distinction on
s^{c}=\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}^{c})
.
If
s^{c}\in S
, then
{Av}(s^{c})={Av}^{c}(s^{c})
and
\mathsf{collapsed}(s^{c})=s^{c}
.
If instead
s^{c}=s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
for some
(R_{i},B_{i})\in\mathsf{EC}
, we have that
a\in\bigcup_{s\in R_{i}}{Av}(s)\setminus B_{i}
.
Thus, there exists a state
s\in R_{i}
such that
s=\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M})
.
But then by definition
\mathsf{collapsed}(s)=s^{c}
.

The following two lemmas show how we can relate paths in the two MDPs with each other.
See
[
61
, Section 6.4.1]
for an alternative view.
Intuitively, the collapsed MDP also gives us a
“quotient”
on the set of paths.
Essentially, a continuous sequence of state-action pairs belonging to the same EC from
\mathsf{EC}
is
“collapsed”
to the corresponding representative.
Vice-versa, any path in the collapsed MDP corresponds to a set of paths in the original MDP.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\varrho=s_{1}a_{1}\dots a_{n-1}s_{n}\in\mathsf{FPaths}_{\mathcal{M}}
be a finite path in the MDP
\mathcal{M}
.
There exists a number
m\leq n
and indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}
with
1\leq i_{j}<i_{j+1}\leq n
such that

\varrho^{c}=\mathsf{collapsed}(s_{i_{1}})a_{i_{1}}\dots a_{i_{m-1}}\mathsf{collapsed}(s_{i_{m}})\in\mathsf{FPaths}_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}

is a finite path in the collapsed MDP
\mathcal{M}^{c}
with
\mathsf{collapsed}(s_{1})=\mathsf{collapsed}(s_{i_{1}})
and
\mathsf{collapsed}(s_{n})=\mathsf{collapsed}(s_{i_{m}})
.

Proof 4.8
.

We construct the path
\varrho^{c}
inductively.
We start with
i_{1}=1
and
s_{1}^{c}=\mathsf{collapsed}(s_{1})
.
Now, either all actions of
\varrho
are in
B_{\mathsf{EC}}
, then by definition of ECs all states of
\varrho
are within the same EC and we are done.
Otherwise, let
a
be the first action along the path
\varrho
such that
a\in Act^{c}
(i.e.
a\notin B_{\mathsf{EC}}
) and let its index equal
j
.
Set
i_{2}=j
,
a_{1}^{c}=a
and
s_{2}^{c}=\mathsf{collapsed}(s_{i+1})
.
Then
a\in{Av}(s_{1}^{c})
.
Repeat the argument with the path
\varrho^{\prime}
equal to the suffix of
\varrho
starting at
j+1
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\varrho^{c}=s^{c}_{1}a^{c}_{1}\dots a^{c}_{m-1}s^{c}_{m}\in\mathsf{FPaths}_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}
be a finite path in the collapsed MDP
\mathcal{M}^{c}
not containing the special states
s_{+}
,
s_{-}
.
There exists a finite path
\varrho=s_{1}a_{1}\dots a_{n-1}s_{n}\in\mathsf{FPaths}_{\mathcal{M}}
in the MDP
\mathcal{M}
with
n\geq m
and indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}
with
1\leq i_{j}<i_{j+1}\leq n
and

•

s_{k}\in\mathsf{states}(s^{c}_{j})
for all
j
and
k
with
i_{j}\leq k<i_{j+1}
(defining
i_{m+1}=n+1
) and

•

if
s^{c}_{j}=s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
then
a_{k}\in B_{i}
for all
j
and
k
with
i_{j}\leq k<i_{j+1}-1
.

Proof 4.10
.

Similar to the above proof, we construct the path
\varrho
inductively.
Distinguish two cases for
s^{c}_{1}
.
If
s^{c}_{1}\in S
, set
s_{1}=s^{c}_{1}
and
a_{1}=a^{c}_{1}
and repeat the argument with the next step of
\varrho^{c}
.
Otherwise, we have that
s^{c}_{1}=s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
for some EC
(R_{i},B_{i})\in\mathsf{EC}
.
Since
(R_{i},B_{i})
is an EC in
\mathcal{M}
, there exists a finite path in
\mathsf{FPaths}_{\mathcal{M}}
only using actions of
B_{i}
from any state in
R_{i}
to
\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c}_{1},\mathcal{M})
.
This path corresponds to the first state-action pair in
\varrho^{c}
.
By definition, there exists a state
s^{\prime}\in S
such that
s^{\prime}\in\support(\Delta(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c}_{1},\mathcal{M}),a^{c}_{1}))
and
\mathsf{collapsed}(s^{\prime})=s^{c}_{2}
.
Thus, we can extend the above path by
a^{c}_{1}s^{\prime}
and repeat the argument.

Based on the previous lemmas, we can establish a correspondence of end components between the original MDP and its (partly) collapsed version.
In particular, for every EC in the original MDP there either exists a single state representing this EC or a new EC in the collapsed MDP.

Lemma 4.11
.

For any EC
(R,B)\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M})
in the MDP
\mathcal{M}
we either have

1.

an EC
(R^{c},B^{c})
in
\mathcal{M}^{c}
, where
R^{c}=\mathsf{collapsed}(R)
and
B^{c}=B\mathbin{\cap}Act^{c}
, or

2.

a state
s_{(R^{\prime},B^{\prime})}\in S^{c}
with
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
and
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
.

Proof 4.12
.

Observe that
Item

2
is trivial by definition, in particular this case is equivalent to
B\subseteq B_{i}
for some
i
.
Moreover,
Item

1
and
Item

2
are mutually exclusive since by construction for any EC
(R_{i},B_{i})
the internal actions
B_{i}
are removed, thus there is no
B\subseteq B_{i}
such that
(\{s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}\},B)
is an EC in
\mathcal{M}^{c}
.

Let thus
(R,B)
be an EC in the MDP
\mathcal{M}
with
B\not\subseteq B_{i}
for all
i
.
We show that
(R^{c},B^{c})
with
R^{c}=\mathsf{collapsed}(R)
and
B^{c}=B\mathbin{\cap}Act^{c}
is an EC in
\mathcal{M}^{c}
.

First, we show by contradiction that
B\not\subseteq B_{\mathsf{EC}}

[Fact I]
, i.e.
B
cannot comprise only internal actions of the ECs in
\mathsf{EC}
.
Recall that by assumption on
\mathsf{EC}
the EC states
R_{i}
are disjoint and
B_{i}
are subsets of the actions enabled in the respective states of
R_{i}
.
Since we assume not to be in
Item

2
,
(R,B)
is an EC with
B\not\subseteq B_{i}
for all
i
.
Assume for contradiction that
B\subseteq B_{\mathsf{EC}}=\bigcup B_{i}
.
Then
(R,B)
necessarily has to contain states of at least two ECs from
\mathsf{EC}
.
Formally, there exist two states
s,s^{\prime}\in R
with
s\in R_{i}
,
s^{\prime}\in R_{j}
, and
i\neq j
.
Since
(R,B)
is an EC, there exists a path from
s
to
s^{\prime}
and vice versa, using only actions from
B
.
As
B\subseteq B_{\mathsf{EC}}
, these actions were available in the ECs before.
Since
s
and
s^{\prime}
are in two ECs with disjoint state sets and a path using only actions from
B
exists between them, there exists a state
s^{\prime\prime}
and action
a\in B\subseteq B_{\mathsf{EC}}
with
\support(\Delta(s^{\prime\prime},a))\not\subseteq R_{i}
.
Since the
a\in B_{\mathsf{EC}}
, we necessarily have
a\in B_{i}
, contradicting the assumption that
(R_{i},B_{i})
is an EC, proving
4.12
.

Next, we prove that
R^{c}=\bigcup_{a\in B^{c}}\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}^{c})

[Fact II]
.
Observe that by assumption we have
R=\bigcup_{a\in B}\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M})
.
By definition of
B^{c}=B\mathbin{\cap}Act^{c}
, we thus have that
\bigcup_{a^{c}\in B^{c}}\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M})\subseteq R
.
Consequently

{\bigcup}_{a^{c}\in B^{c}}\mathsf{collapsed}(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M}))\subseteq\mathsf{collapsed}(R)=R^{c}

Applying
Lemma

4.5
yields

{\bigcup}_{a^{c}\in B^{c}}\mathsf{collapsed}(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M}))={\bigcup}_{a^{c}\in B^{c}}\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M}^{c}),

thus
\bigcup_{a^{c}\in B^{c}}\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M}^{c})\subseteq R^{c}
.

Now, assume for contradiction that there exists a state
s^{c}\in R^{c}
such that
s^{c}\neq\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M}^{c})
for all
a^{c}\in B^{c}
.
Due to the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{c}
, we either have
s^{c}\in S
,
s^{c}=s_{(R^{\prime},B^{\prime})}
for some EC
(R^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in\mathsf{EC}
, or
s^{c}\in\{s_{+},s_{-}\}
.
The third case immediately leads to a contradiction, since
B^{c}\subseteq Act
and thus
a_{+},a_{-}\notin B^{c}
.
In the first case, we have that
s^{c}\notin R_{i}
for any
i
, thus
{Av}(s^{c})={Av}^{c}(s^{c})\subseteq Act^{c}
.
Hence, any action
a
of this state contained in the EC
(R,B)
is still available in the collapsed MDP and thus also contained in the EC
(R^{c},B^{c})
.
The second case implies, by definition of
R^{c}=\mathsf{collapsed}(R)
, that there exists an EC
(R_{i},B_{i})\in\mathsf{EC}
such that
R_{i}\mathbin{\cap}R\neq\emptyset
.
Recall that
{Av}^{c}(s_{(R_{i},B_{i})})=\bigcup_{s\in R_{i}}{Av}(s)\setminus B_{i}
.
The case assumption is thus equivalent to
B^{c}\mathbin{\cap}(\bigcup_{s\in R_{i}}{Av}(s)\setminus B_{i})=\emptyset
.
Inserting the definition of
B^{c}
and
Act^{c}
yields

B\mathbin{\cap}(Act\setminus B_{\mathsf{EC}})\mathbin{\cap}({\bigcup}_{s\in R_{i}}{Av}(s)\setminus B_{i})=B\mathbin{\cap}({\bigcup}_{s\in R_{i}}{Av}(s)\setminus B_{i})=\\
{\bigcup}_{s\in R_{i}\mathbin{\cap}R}{Av}(s)\mathbin{\cap}B\setminus B_{i}=\emptyset.

This implies that
{Av}(s)\mathbin{\cap}B\subseteq B_{i}
for all
s\in R_{i}\mathbin{\cap}R
, i.e. all such states only have
“internal”
actions of the EC
(R_{i},B_{i})
available in
(R,B)
.
But this implies
R\subseteq R_{i}
and
B\subseteq B_{i}
, contradicting our assumptions.
This concludes the proof of
4.12
.

Now, we prove that
(R^{c},B^{c})
is a proper EC in
\mathcal{M}^{c}
, i.e. that (i)
R^{c}\neq\emptyset
,
\emptyset\neq B^{c}\subseteq\bigcup_{s^{c}\in R^{c}}{Av}(s^{c})
, (ii) for all
s^{c}\in R^{c}
,
a\in B^{c}\mathbin{\cap}{Av}^{c}(s^{c})
we have
\support(\Delta^{c}(s^{c},a^{c}))\subseteq R^{c}
, and (iii) for all states
s^{c},s^{\prime c}\in R^{c}
there exists a path from
s^{c}
to
s^{\prime c}
only using actions from
B^{c}
.

For (i), we have
B^{c}\neq\emptyset
, otherwise
B^{c}=B\mathbin{\cap}Act^{c}=\emptyset
implies
B\subseteq B_{\mathsf{EC}}
, contradicting
4.12
.

4.12
yields the second part of the first condition.

To prove (ii), assume a contradiction, i.e. let
s^{c}\in R^{c}
,
a\in B^{c}\mathbin{\cap}{Av}^{c}(s^{c})
such that
s^{\prime c}\in\support(\Delta^{c}(s^{c},a^{c}))\setminus R^{c}
.
Let
s=\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M})
(implying
s^{c}=\mathsf{collapsed}(s)
).
Again, we proceed by a case distinction, this time on the successor
s^{\prime c}
.
If
s^{\prime c}\in S
, we have that
s^{\prime c}\in\support(\Delta(s,a^{c}))
, since
s\in R
and
a^{c}\in B
and
(R,B)
is an EC.
Further,
\Delta^{c}(s^{c},a^{c},s^{\prime c})=\Delta(s^{c},a^{c},s^{\prime c})
, thus
s^{\prime c}\in\support(\Delta^{c}(s^{c},a^{c}))
, contradicting the assumption.
If instead
s^{\prime c}=s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
, then there exists a state
s^{\prime}\in\support(\Delta(s,a^{c}))\mathbin{\cap}R_{i}
by definition of
\Delta^{c}
.
But then
s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}\in R^{c}
by definition of
R^{c}
, contradiction.

Finally, to show (iii), we can directly apply
Lemma

4.7
to obtain the required path as follows.
Let
s^{c},s^{\prime c}\in R^{c}
two states and pick two arbitrary
s,s^{\prime}\in R
with
\mathsf{collapsed}(s)=s^{c}
and
\mathsf{collapsed}(s^{\prime})=s^{\prime c}
.
Since
(R,B)
is an EC, there exists a finite path
\varrho
from
s
to
s^{\prime}
, using only actions of
B
.
By
Lemma

4.7
, we get a path
\varrho^{c}
from
s^{c}
to
s^{\prime c}
using only actions from
B\mathbin{\cap}Act^{c}=B^{c}
, concluding the proof of
Item

1
.

As expected, the corresponding reverse statement holds true, too, i.e. every EC in the collapsed MDP yields a corresponding EC in the original MDP.

Lemma 4.13
.

For all ECs
(R^{c},B^{c})
in
\mathcal{M}^{c}
with
s_{+},s_{-}\notin R^{c}
we have that
(R,B)
with
R=\mathsf{states}(R^{c})
and
B=B^{c}\mathbin{\cup}\bigcup_{s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}\in R^{c}}B_{i}
is an EC in
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof 4.14
.

Fix an EC
(R^{c},B^{c})
in
\mathcal{M}^{c}
and set
R=\mathsf{states}(R^{c})
and
B=B^{c}\mathbin{\cup}\bigcup_{s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}\in R^{c}}B_{i}
.
We need to prove that
(R,B)
is an EC in
\mathcal{M}
.
Clearly,
R
and
B
are non-empty.
We show that
R=\bigcup_{a\in B}\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M})
.
For any
s\in R
, there exists a
s^{c}\in R^{c}
such that
s\in\mathsf{states}(s^{c})
by definition of
R
.
If
s=s^{c}
we have
s\in R^{c}
and there exists an action
a^{c}\in B^{c}\subseteq B
with
\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{c},\mathcal{M})=s
.
Otherwise, there is an EC
(R_{i},B_{i})\in\mathsf{EC}
with
s\in R_{i}
,
s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}\in R^{c}
, and, since
(R_{i},B_{i})
is in EC in
\mathcal{M}
, there is an action
a\in B_{i}\subseteq B
with
\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M})=s
.
Similarly, for any action
a\in B
we have that
\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M})\in R
by analogous reasoning.

It remains to show that (i) for all
s\in R
,
a\in B\mathbin{\cap}{Av}(s)
we have
\support(\Delta(s,a))\subseteq R
, and (ii) for all
s,s^{\prime}\in R
there is a finite path from
s
to
s^{\prime}
only using actions from
B
.
For (i), we again assume contradiction, i.e. there are states
s\in R
,
s^{\prime}\in S
and an action
a\in{Av}(s)\mathbin{\cap}B
such that
s^{\prime}\in\support(\Delta(s,a))\setminus R
.
We again proceed by case distinctions, but now first on
a
.
If
a\in B^{c}
, then
\support(\Delta^{c}(\mathsf{collapsed}(s),a))\subseteq R^{c}
, as
(R^{c},B^{c})
is an EC.
By definition of
\Delta^{c}
, we have
\mathsf{collapsed}(s^{\prime})\in\support(\Delta^{c}(\mathsf{collapsed}(s),a))
.
Together, this implies
s^{\prime}\in R
, yielding a contradiction.
If instead
a\in B_{i}
for some EC
(R_{i},B_{i})\in\mathsf{EC}
, then
s,s^{\prime}\in R_{i}\subseteq R
, also leading to a contradiction.
Finally, to prove (ii), we can directly apply
Lemma

4.9
to a path from
\mathsf{collapsed}(s)
to
\mathsf{collapsed}(s^{\prime})
in
(R^{c},B^{c})
, yielding a path from
s
to
s^{\prime}
in
(R,B)
.

The previous statement implies that if we collapse a MEC of the original MDP, then there can be no EC in the collapsed MDP containing the MEC representative state.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\{(R^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}\subseteq\mathsf{EC}\mathbin{\cap}\MecsOp(\mathcal{M})
be some MECs of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathsf{EC}
.
Then, we have that
s_{(R^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})}\notin R^{c}
for any EC
(R^{c},B^{c})
in
\mathcal{M}^{c}
.

Proof 4.16
.

Assume there is such an EC
(R^{c},B^{c})
with
s_{(R^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})}\in R^{c}
.

Lemma

4.13
yields an EC
(R,B)
with
R^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq R
,
B^{\prime}_{i}\subsetneq B
, contradiction to
(R,B)
being a MEC in
\mathcal{M}
.

The statement of
Lemma

4.15
does not hold for any EC
(R^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})\in\mathsf{EC}
, since there might be a larger EC containing
s_{(R^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})}
.
For example, in
Figure

4
, the collapsed MDP has an EC containing representative states.
However, if all MECs are collapsed, the resulting collapsed MDP indeed has no ECs except two trivial ones.

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{c}=\collapse(\mathcal{M},\MecsOp(\mathcal{M}),\hat{s},T)
be the collapsed MDP of
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathsf{EC}=\MecsOp(\mathcal{M})
.
Then,
\mathcal{M}^{c}
satisfies
1
.

Proof 4.18
.

Follows directly from the above
Lemma

4.15
.

Finally, we also get that the reachability probabilities are preserved.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{c}=(S^{c},Act^{c},{Av}^{c},\Delta^{c})=\collapse(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{EC},\hat{s},T)
be the collapsed MDP of
\mathcal{M}
, where
\mathsf{EC}=\{(R_{i},B_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
is any appropriate set of end components.
Then, for any state
s\in S
it holds that

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\max}[\Diamond T]=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{c},\mathsf{collapsed}(s)}^{\max}[\Diamond\mathsf{collapsed}(T)]=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{c},\mathsf{collapsed}(s)}^{\max}[\Diamond(\{s_{+}\}\mathbin{\cup}(T\mathbin{\cap}S^{c}))].

Proof 4.20
.

First, observe that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{c},s^{c}}^{\max}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]=1
for any state
s^{c}=s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
with
R_{i}\mathbin{\cap}T\neq\emptyset
by definition.
Moreover,
T\mathbin{\cap}S^{c}=T\setminus R_{\mathsf{EC}}
, i.e. all target states which are not part of an EC in
\mathsf{EC}
.
Every state
s^{c}\in\mathsf{collapsed}(T)
is of one of these two kinds.
Hence,
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{c},\mathsf{collapsed}(s)}^{\max}[\Diamond(\{s_{+}\}\mathbin{\cup}(T\mathbin{\cap}S^{c}))]=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{c},\mathsf{collapsed}(s)}^{\max}[\Diamond\mathsf{collapsed}(T)]
, proving the second equality.

For the first equality, we argue how to transform the witness strategies, achieving the same overall reachability probability.
Thus, let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{MD}}
be a (memoryless deterministic) strategy in
\mathcal{M}
maximizing the probability of reaching
T
.
We define a strategy
\pi^{c}
on
\mathcal{M}^{c}
simulating
\pi
as follows.
Note that
\pi^{c}
does not have to be memoryless or deterministic.
For all states
s^{c}\in S
, i.e.
s^{c}
is not a collapsed representative,
\pi^{c}
mimics
\pi
, i.e.
\pi^{c}(s)=\pi(s)
.
For the other case, namely
s^{c}=s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
for some EC
(R_{i},B_{i})\in\mathsf{EC}
, recall that
\pi^{c}
is allowed to have memory.
In particular, it can remember the action
a
leading to
s^{c}
.
Clearly, for any such action
a
and other action
a^{\prime}\in{Av}^{c}(s^{c})
we can compute the probability of
a^{\prime}
action being the first action not in
B_{i}
under
\pi
.
Then,
\pi^{c}
simply selects
a^{\prime}
in
s^{c}
after
a
with this probability.
Moreover, we also need to compute the probability of remaining inside
R_{i}
forever, which corresponds to the probability of
\pi^{c}
choosing
\mathrm{rem}_{i}
.
It is easy to see that
\pi^{c}
achieves the same reachability as
\pi
.

If we instead start with a strategy in the collapsed MDP
\pi^{c}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}^{\mathsf{MD}}
, we construct the respective strategy
\pi
on
\mathcal{M}
as follows.
Again, on states
s\notin R_{\mathsf{EC}}
, we simply replicate the choice of
\pi^{c}
.
On states
s_{(R_{i},B_{i})}
the strategy
\pi^{c}
chooses a single action
a^{c}\in{Av}^{c}(s_{(R_{i},B_{i})})
, since it is deterministic.
If that action is
\mathrm{rem}_{i}
,
\pi
simply picks any internal
a\in B_{i}
in each state
R_{i}
.
Otherwise, there exists a strategy
\pi^{\prime}
on
R_{i}
reaching state
\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M})
with probability
1
.
Thus,
\pi
mimics
\pi^{\prime}
until that state is reached and then plays
a^{c}
, again achieving the same reachability.

4.2
The General BRTDP Algorithm

Now, we present our modification of
Algorithm

2
, using the idea of collapsing, to obtain the general approach as shown in
Algorithm

3
.
On top of the previously presented ideas, the algorithm maintains a growing set of ECs and repeatedly collapses the input MDP.

14

1

Input:

MDP
\mathcal{M}
, state
\hat{s}
, target set
T
, precision
\varepsilon
, initial bounds
\mathsf{Up}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}
, and initial set of ECs
\EcsOp_{1}
.

Output:

\varepsilon
-optimal values
(l,u)
, i.e.,
\val(\hat{s})\in[l,u]
and
0\leq u-l<\varepsilon
.

2

\mathsf{e}\leftarrow 1
,
\mathcal{M}^{c}_{1}\leftarrow\collapse(\mathcal{M},\EcsOp_{1},\hat{s},T)

3

\mathsf{Up}_{1}(s_{+},a_{+})\leftarrow 1
,
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}(s_{+},a_{+})\leftarrow 1
,
\mathsf{Up}_{1}(s_{-},a_{-})\leftarrow 0
,
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}(s_{-},a_{-})=0

4

5

while

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s})\geq\varepsilon

do

6

forall

(R_{j},B_{j})\in\EcsOp_{\mathsf{e}}

do

//
Initialize bounds of representative states

7

forall

a\in{Av}(s_{(R_{j},B_{j})})\setminus\{\mathrm{rem}_{j}\}

do

//
Copy bounds for existing actions

8

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{(R_{j},B_{j})},a)\leftarrow\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}),a)

9

\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{(R_{j},B_{j})},a)\leftarrow\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}),a)

10

if

R_{j}\mathbin{\cap}T=\emptyset

then

//
Set bounds for remain action

11

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{(R_{j},B_{j})},\mathrm{rem}_{j})\leftarrow 0
,
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{(R_{j},B_{j})},\mathrm{rem}_{j})\leftarrow 0

12

else

13

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{(R_{j},B_{j})},\mathrm{rem}_{j})\leftarrow 1
,
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{(R_{j},B_{j})},\mathrm{rem}_{j})\leftarrow 1

14

15

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}+1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}+1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}

16

\varrho\leftarrow\textsc{SamplePairs}(\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s},\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}},\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}},\varepsilon)

//
Sample a path in collapsed MDP

forall

(s,a)\in\varrho

do

//
Update the upper and lower bounds

17

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s,a)\leftarrow\Delta(s,a)\langle\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}\rangle

18

\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s,a)\leftarrow\Delta(s,a)\langle\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}\rangle

19

\EcsOp_{\mathsf{e}+1}\leftarrow\textsc{UpdateECs}(\mathcal{M},\EcsOp_{\mathsf{e}})

//
Search for new ECs

\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}+1}\leftarrow\collapse(\mathcal{M},\EcsOp_{\mathsf{e}+1},\hat{s},T)

//
Update the collapsed MDP

\mathsf{e}\leftarrow\mathsf{e}+1

20

21

return

(\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s}),\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s}))

22

Algorithm 3

The BRTDP learning algorithm for general MDPs.

The new auxiliary procedure
UpdateECs
is supposed to identify ECs in
\mathcal{M}
.
As with
SamplePairs
, we only require some properties instead of giving a concrete implementation.
Essentially,
UpdateECs
should only grow its list of ECs and eventually identify all ECs which are repeatedly visited by
SamplePairs
.

Assumption 4
.

Let
\mathsf{EC}_{1}\subseteq\EcsOp(\mathcal{M})
be an initial set of state-disjoint ECs,
\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}+1}=\textsc{UpdateECs}(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}})
the identified ECs, and
\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}=\collapse(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s},T)
the corresponding collapsed MDPs.
Then, for any episode
\mathsf{e}
and EC
(R,B)\in\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
(R,B)
is an EC of
\mathcal{M}
and there exists
(R^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}+1}
with
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
and
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
.

This is, for example, easily satisfied by searching for ECs in the set of visited states in every step.
However, an efficient implementation may want to choose the times when it actually searches heuristically.

Since there are only finitely many states, this assumption implies that eventually
\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}}
and thus
\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}
stabilizes, i.e. there exists some episode
\overline{\mathsf{e}}
such that for all
\mathsf{e}\geq\overline{\mathsf{e}}
we have that
\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}}=\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}+1}
and thus
\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}=\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}+1}
.
We call
\overline{\mathsf{e}}
the
EC-stable episode
.

Assumption 5
.

Let
\mathsf{EC}_{\mathsf{e}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}
as in
4
and assume that assumption holds.
Further, let
\varrho_{\mathsf{e}}\in\mathsf{FPaths}_{\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}}
be an infinite series of sets of state-action pairs in
\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}
and define
S^{c}_{\infty}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{\mathsf{e}=k}^{\infty}\{s\in S^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}\mid s\in\varrho^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}\}
the set of states occurring infinitely often.
9
9

9

As mentioned above, due to
4
we get a EC-stable episode
\overline{\mathsf{e}}
and thus have
S^{c}_{\infty}\subseteq S_{\overline{\mathsf{e}}}^{c}
, i.e. the set of infinitely often seen states are all states of
\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{e}}}^{c}
.

Then, there exists no EC
(R^{c},B^{c})
in
\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\overline{\mathsf{e}}}
with
R^{c}\subseteq S_{\infty}^{c}
except
R^{c}=\{s_{+}\}
or
R^{c}=\{s_{-}\}
.

4.3
Proof of Correctness

We now continue to prove correctness and termination of
Algorithm

3
.
First, we argue that the algorithm indeed is well-defined, i.e. it never accesses undefined values.

Lemma 4.21
.

Algorithm

3
is well-defined.

Proof 4.22
.

We only need to show that the states introduced by the collapsing in
Algorithms

3
and
3
are assigned bounds before being accessed.
By definition of the collapsed MDP, we add a state for each EC together with an additional action, and the special states
\{s_{+},s_{-}\}
.
The initial collapse in
Algorithm

3
adds the special states together with their corresponding actions.
Their values are initialised in the following line.
Furthermore, the EC collapsing in
Algorithms

3
and
3
adds a state
s_{(R,B)}
for any EC
(R,B)\in\EcsOp_{\mathsf{e}}
and a corresponding
\mathrm{rem}
action.
Their values are initialised in
Algorithms

3
and
3
and not accessed prior to that.

As in
2
, we again assume that the initial inputs are correct.

Assumption 6
.

The given initial bounds
\mathsf{Up}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}
are correct, i.e.
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}(s,a)\leq\val(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Up}_{1}(s,a)
for all
s\in S,a\in{Av}(s)
.
Furthermore, the given initial set of ECs is correct, i.e.
\mathsf{EC}_{1}\subseteq\EcsOp(\mathcal{M})
and pairwise disjoint.

Lemma 4.23
.

Assume that
6
holds.
Then, during any execution of
Algorithm

3
we have for every episode
\mathsf{e}
, all states
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
and action
a\in{Av}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
that

\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s,a)\leq\val(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a).

Proof 4.24
.

We prove that the initialization of values for newly added states is correct.
The remaining proof then is completely analogous to the proof of
Lemma

3.2
.

Since
s_{+}
is the target in
\mathcal{M}^{c}
, setting
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}(s_{+},a_{+})=1
is correct.
Analogously, we see that
s_{-}
has no outgoing action and thus cannot reach
s_{+}
, justifying
\mathsf{Up}_{1}(s_{-},a_{-})=0
.

The correctness of updates for the collapsed states follows from
Lemma

4.19
.

Lemma 4.25
.

The result of
Algorithm

3
is correct under
6
, i.e. (i)
0\leq u-l<\varepsilon
, and (ii)
\val(\hat{s})\in[l,u]
.

Proof 4.26
.

As in
Lemma

3.4
, the claims follows from the algorithm and
Lemma

4.23
.

Finally, we can prove termination of our presented algorithm.
The proof is very similar to the proof of
Lemma

3.6
and we only need to incorporate the new assumptions about
UpdateECs
.

Lemma 4.27
.

Algorithm

3
terminates under
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
.
It terminates almost surely if
3
is satisfied almost surely.

Proof 4.28
.

We apply the same reasoning as in
Lemma

3.6
until
1
is applied in the final part of the proof.
Since we do not necessarily explore all of
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{M}^{c}_{\mathsf{e}}
may still contain MECs.
In the proof,
1
is used only to show that
S_{\mathrm{Diff}}\subseteq S_{\infty}
does not contain MECs.
Observe that any non-terminating execution eventually reaches an EC-stable episode
\overline{\mathsf{e}}
, thus the collapsed MDP considered by the algorithm does not change.
Now,
S_{\infty}
in the previous proof exactly corresponds to
S_{\infty}^{c}
of
5
, which yields that again there is no EC in
S_{\infty}^{c}
.
Thus, we can continue to apply the previous proof’s reasoning.

Again, we get the overall soundness as a direct consequence.

Theorem 4.29
.

Assume that (almost surely)
3
, as well as
4
,
5
and
6
hold.
Then
Algorithm

3
is correct and converges (almost surely).

4.4
Relation to Interval Iteration

We briefly outline how our BRTDP algorithm presented in
Algorithm

3
generalizes both the original BRTDP algorithm of
[
33
]
and the interval iteration algorithm of
[
73
]
.
To this end, we give a brief overview of interval iteration.
The algorithm first identifies all MECs and constructs a quotient similar to the one we presented in
Section

4.1
.
Then, each state is initialised with straightforward upper and lower bounds.
These bounds then are iterated globally according to the Bellman operator.
We can emulate this behaviour by directly yielding the set of all MECs in
UpdateECs
and returning
S^{c}\times{Av}^{c}
on each call to
SamplePairs
.
All variants of
[
33
]
can be obtained by choosing the appropriate path sampling heuristics for
SamplePairs
.

5
Limited Information – MDP without End Components

We adapt our approach to the setting of limited information, where we can access the system only as a
“black box”
and we are given some bounds on the shape of the system (see
Section

2.3
).
Intuitively, since we are interested in an
\varepsilon
-precise solution, we can repeatedly sample the system to learn the transition probabilities with high confidence.
By adapting our previous ideas, we can enhance this approach to only learn
“interesting”
transitions.
Since we can never bound the transition probabilities with absolute certainty, we aim for a
probably approximately correct
algorithm, which gives an
\varepsilon
-optimal solution with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Relevance and Applicability

Before we go into the details, we discuss the purpose and motivation for the subsequent algorithms.
As mentioned in the introduction, our primary aim is to provide a
possibility result
, showing that it is possible to obtain PAC bounds on the
maximal
value on
infinite horizon
reachability values in a
black box
setting, only using samples of
finite length
and only starting in the
initial state
, and all this for
general MDP
(with ECs)
even in a model-free setting
(see below for a brief comment on model-free).
Due to this focus, the bounds that the presented approaches obtain are rather impractical and of mostly theoretical value.
This can be alleviated in several ways.
For one, tighter statistical methods could be used, see
[
116
]
for a recent discussion (we use the naïve Hoeffding’s inequality to simplify proofs).
Additionally, our approach is generic in the sense that it assumes the worst of the system.
Specific knowledge about the model, e.g. (in-)dependence of states, could be incorporated to significantly improve practical scalability.
Yet, these points are orthogonal to our aim of proving the possibility of (model-free) PAC, for which we provide a complete proof in the following.
Moreover, an additional aim of this work is to provide a re-usable framework for proofs in this direction.
We believe that several statements in the proofs below might be useful for other endeavours of this kind, especially the auxiliary statements in
Appendix

A
.

Remark 5.1
.

Intuitively, the idea of
“model-free”
is that such approaches do not try to learn the concrete transition probabilities or the entire graph structure, but more
“compressed”
quantities such as state- or action-values.
Indeed, our algorithm only stores a fixed number of values per state-action pair, not for each transition.
In most literature, model-free is only loosely defined, as it is difficult to formalize precisely
[
129
]
.
In
[
129
, Definition 1]
, the authors try to capture model-free by requiring that the space complexity of an approach should be
o(|S|^{2}|Act|)
(in other words, less than the explicit graph representation of the MDP).
At the same time, the space complexity naturally also depends on parameters such as
\varepsilon
and
\delta
(e.g. suppose that
\varepsilon
were of exponential size w.r.t. the entire system).
As such, we are interested in the above complexity for
fixed
parameters.
The estimates our algorithm obtains are based on repeated updates to action values.
Later, in
Lemma

5.3
, we show that (for fixed parameters) the number of executed updates is bounded by
\lvert Act\rvert
, and thus one can prove that the updates only involves numbers that are of size
\lvert Act\rvert
.
In any case, proving that our approach formally satisfies (one of the many) definition of model-free is not our main goal, but rather observing that it captures the
“spirit”
of model-free by not learning probabilities but rather values directly.

For a model-based approach to this problem, we direct the reader to
[
10
,
116
]
.
These approaches essentially obtain bounds on every single transition probability in the system and then solve the induced
interval MDP
to obtain bounds on the value.

5.1
Definition of Limited Information

We define the limited information setting.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,Act,{Av},\Delta)
be some MDP,
\hat{s}\in S
a starting state, and
T\subseteq S
a target set.
An algorithm has
limited information
if it can access

•

the starting state
\hat{s}
,

•

a target oracle for
T
, i.e. given a state
s
it can query whether
s\in T
,

•

an upper bound
A
of the number of actions,
A\geq\lvert Act\rvert
,

•

a lower bound
q
on the transition probabilities under any uniform strategy,
0<q\leq p_{\min}=\min\{\lvert{Av}(s)\rvert^{-1}\cdot\Delta(s,a,s^{\prime})\mid s\in S,a\in{Av}(s),s^{\prime}\in\support(\Delta(s,a))\}
,

•

an oracle for the set of available actions
{Av}
, and

•

a successor oracle
\mathsf{succ}
, which given a state-action pair yields a successor state, sampled according to the underlying, hidden probability distribution
\Delta
.

To tackle this problem, we combine the BRTDP approach with
delayed Q-learning
(DQL)
[
129
]
.
In essence, DQL temporarily accumulates sampled values for each state-action pair and only attempts an update after a certain delay, i.e. after enough samples have been gathered for a particular pair.
Intuitively, with a large enough delay, the average of the sampled values is close to the true average with high confidence.
Moreover, the attempted update is only successful if the value is changed by at least some margin.
If instead the update fails, another update is only allowed if any other value in the system has changed significantly.
This way, we can bound the total number of attempted updates and thus control the overall probability of any
“wrong”
update occurring.
We explain all these ideas in more detail later on.

5.2
The No-EC DQL Algorithm

First, we again restrict ourselves to the case of no end components, as these pose an additional difficulty.
Thus, we assume the MDP
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
1
and instead of a target state oracle, the algorithm is explicitly given the special states
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
.
We present our DQL-based approach in
Algorithm

4
.
While it is similar in spirit to
Algorithm

2
, we give a concrete instantiation of
SamplePairs
, since this setting needs a lot of additional guarantees.

Input:

Inputs as given in
Definition

5.2
satisfying
1
, special states
s_{+},s_{-}
, precision
\varepsilon
, and confidence
\delta
.

Output:

Values
(l,u)
which are
\varepsilon
-optimal, i.e.,
\val(\hat{s})\in[l,u]
and
0\leq u-l<\varepsilon
, with probability at least
1-\delta
.

\mathsf{Up}_{1}(\cdot,\cdot)\leftarrow 1
,
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}(\cdot,\cdot)\leftarrow 0
,
\mathsf{Up}_{1}(s_{-},\cdot)\leftarrow 0
,
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}(s_{+},\cdot)\leftarrow 1

1

for

\circ\in\{\mathsf{Up},\mathsf{Lo}\}

do

2

\texttt{learn}_{1}^{\circ}(\cdot,\cdot)\leftarrow\texttt{yes}
,
\texttt{acc}_{1}^{\circ}(\cdot,\cdot)\leftarrow 0
,
\texttt{count}_{1}^{\circ}(\cdot,\cdot)\leftarrow 0

3

\mathsf{e}\leftarrow 1
,
\mathsf{t}\leftarrow 1

4

5

while

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})\geq\varepsilon

do

6

for

s\in S

do

\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\leftarrow\argmax_{a\in{Av}(s)}\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)

7

s_{\mathsf{t}}\leftarrow\hat{s}

8

while

s_{\mathsf{t}}\notin\{s_{+},s_{-}\}

do

//
Experience the current learning episode

9

a_{\mathsf{t}}\leftarrow
sampled uniformly from
\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{\mathsf{t}})

//
Pick an action

s_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{succ}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})

//
Query successor oracle

10

/*
Update bound estimates */

for

\circ\in\{\mathsf{Up},\mathsf{Lo}\}

do

11

if

\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\neq\texttt{no}

then

12

\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\circ}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})+1

13

\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\circ}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})+\bigcirc_{\mathsf{t}}(s_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime})

14

15

/*
Learn upper bounds */

if

\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})={\overline{m}}

then

//
Attempt update of
\mathsf{Up}

16

if

\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})/{\overline{m}}<\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})-2{\overline{\varepsilon}}

then

17

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})/{\overline{m}}+{\overline{\varepsilon}}

//
Successful update

\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}(\cdot,\cdot)\leftarrow\texttt{yes}

//
Re-enable learning for all actions

else

18

\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow\textsc{Decrease}(\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}}))

//
Failed update

\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow 0
,
\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow 0

19

20

/*
Learn lower bounds */

if

\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Lo}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})={\overline{m}}

then

//
Attempt update of
\mathsf{Lo}

21

if

\texttt{acc}^{\mathsf{Lo}}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})/{\overline{m}}>\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})+2{\overline{\varepsilon}}

then

22

\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow\texttt{acc}^{\mathsf{Lo}}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})/{\overline{m}}-{\overline{\varepsilon}}

//
Successful update

\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Lo}}(\cdot,\cdot)\leftarrow\texttt{yes}

//
Re-enable learning for all actions

else

23

\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Lo}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow\textsc{Decrease}(\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}}))

//
Failed update

\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Lo}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow 0
,
\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Lo}}(s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}})\leftarrow 0

24

25

s_{\mathsf{t}+1}\leftarrow s_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{t}\leftarrow\mathsf{t}+1

//
Increase step counter

\mathsf{e}\leftarrow\mathsf{e}+1

//
Increase episode counter

return

(\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s}),\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s}))

26

Algorithm 4

The DQL learning algorithm for MDPs without ECs.

The algorithm contains several auxiliary variables.
Most are values kept for each state-action pair, and separate for both the upper and lower bound.
We give a brief intuition for each variable, where
\circ\in\{\mathsf{Up},\mathsf{Lo}\}
and
(s,a)
is a state-action pair in
\mathcal{M}
:

•

\mathsf{t}
: The number of steps the algorithm took so far, increased by 1 after each iteration of the main loop, as already mentioned in the preliminaries.

•

s_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{t}},s^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}
:
The state, action, and the sampled successor state in step
\mathsf{t}
, respectively.

•

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
and
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
:
The (estimated) upper and lower bounds for the state-action pair
(s,a)
at step
\mathsf{t}
.
Note that in contrast to the previous algorithm, the upper and lower bounds are updated at each step instead of each episode.

•

\texttt{learn}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
:
A three-valued flag (
yes
,
once
, or
no
) indicating whether the algorithm currently tries to learn and update the
\circ
-bounds for
(s,a)
.
The meaning of
once
is explained in the following.
We additionally use the
Decrease
function for convenience, which is defined by
\texttt{yes}\mapsto\texttt{once}
,
\texttt{once}\mapsto\texttt{no}
, and
\texttt{no}\mapsto\texttt{no}
.

•

\texttt{count}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
:
The number of times a value for
(s,a)
was experienced.
When
\texttt{count}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
is large enough, we can attempt an update with sufficient confidence.

•

\texttt{acc}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
:
The accumulated sampled values of the last
\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}(s,a)
visits to
(s,a)
.
We want
\texttt{acc}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)/\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}(s,a)
to approximate the true
\circ
-bound.

Moreover, the algorithm contains the two constants
{\overline{\varepsilon}}
and
{\overline{m}}
.
We define their value (and the value of another constant, used for readability) as follows.

Figure 5:
Example MDP to explain the choices and interpretations of some constants.

{\overline{\varepsilon}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot\frac{p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}}{3\lvert S\rvert}\qquad{\overline{\xi}}=2\lvert Act\rvert\left(1+\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}\right)\qquad{\overline{m}}=\left\lceil\frac{1}{2{\overline{\varepsilon}}^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{8}{\delta}{\overline{\xi}}\right)\right\rceil

We call
{\overline{\varepsilon}}
the
update step
(the smallest update increment considered significant by the algorithm),
{\overline{\xi}}
the
update count
(the maximal possible number of update attempts, mainly introduced for readability), and
{\overline{m}}
the
update delay
(the number of samples we want to obtain for a state-action pair before we attempt an update).
These three constants are used throughout this and the following section.
Note that bounds on these constants can be obtained from
Definition

5.2
(recalling that
\lvert Act\rvert
is an upper bound on
\lvert S\rvert
).
Within the proofs, an even smaller value for
{\overline{\varepsilon}}
or an even larger value for
{\overline{m}}
are also sufficient.
We define the constants with
“tight”
values to aid readability and intuition.

These constants are closely related to the worst-case
mixing rate
(see e.g.
[
111
, Chapter 5]
for a detailed discussion) of the MDP, which intuitively indicates how fast information
“propagates”
through the system.
For Markov chains, this is given by the difference between first and second eigenvalue of the transition matrix, which is also called
spectral gap
.
This gap can be (quite conservatively) bounded by
p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
.
This also gives a bound on the convergence rate of the
power iteration
, which in the context of Markov chains and MDP is closely related to value iteration.
(See, for example,
[
118
, Theorem 8.5.2]
, noting that
p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
is a lower bound for
\eta
with
J=\lvert S\rvert
.)

The concept of information propagation (and the tightness of the
p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
bound) is illustrated in
Figure

5
.
In order to propagate any information about state
s_{n}
to the initial state
\hat{s}
, we need
\lvert S\rvert
steps.
Moreover, after this many steps only a fraction
p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
of the information is propagated, so, intuitively, to
“observe”
a difference of
\varepsilon
, we need to perform
\approx\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}/\varepsilon
steps.
Thus, we need to visit a state-action pair often enough, i.e.
{\overline{m}}
times, before an update to ensure that relevant information has propagated already with high confidence.
Dually, if a state-action pair was visited often enough and new information does not differ from the previous information by more than
{\overline{\varepsilon}}
, there likely is no new information to be propagated and we may assume that the values of this state-action pair have converged.

Inside the main loop, the algorithm repeats two steps to obtain a path.
First, an action maximizing the upper bounds (at the beginning of the episode) is randomly picked.
More precisely, we again consider the set
\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\coloneqq\argmax_{a\in{Av}(s)}\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{e}}}(s,a)
and uniformly select an action thereof.
To obtain the successor, we query the successor oracle with the given action to obtain the successor
s^{\prime}
.
In other words, in episode
\mathsf{e}
the algorithm samples a path in the MDP using a memoryless strategy randomizing uniformly over
\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
in each state.
We call this strategy the
sampling strategy

\pi_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\coloneqq\lvert\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\rvert^{-1}
if
a\in\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
and
0
otherwise.
We will later on introduce the upper bound maximizing strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
, which selects among
\mathsf{Up}
-optimal actions at the current step
\mathsf{t}
.
Note that if the algorithm follows this strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
while sampling, the samples would not be obtained from a memoryless strategy in general, since an update might happen while sampling and thus change the strategy.
One might be tempted to solve this issue by first sampling a path until
s_{+}
or
s_{-}
is reached and then propagating the values.
However this path might be of exponential size w.r.t. the number of states; this already occurs for the structurally simple example in
Figure

5
.

After sampling a tuple
(s,a,s^{\prime})
, the algorithm learns from this
“experience”
.
It does so by learning upper and lower bounds separately, depending on the respective
learn
flags, which are explained later.
In case one of the bounds should be learned (
\texttt{learn}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)\neq\texttt{no}
), the accumulator is updated with the newly observed values, i.e. the respective bound of the successor
s^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, if the algorithm has gathered enough information, i.e. this pair has been experienced
{\overline{m}}
times, an update of
(s,a)
’s estimate is attempted (if the respective
learn
is
yes
or
once
).
By choosing
{\overline{m}}
large enough, the information we gathered about the bounds of
(s,a)
very likely is a faithful approximation of the true expected value over its successors.
If the newly learned estimate, i.e. the average over the last
{\overline{m}}
experiences of
(s,a)
, significantly differs from the current estimate stored in
\mathsf{Up}
or
\mathsf{Lo}
, the current estimates are updated conservatively.
If instead this new estimate is close to the current estimate, the algorithm marks this state-action pair as (potentially) converged by
“decreasing”
its
learn
flag, as specified by
Decrease
.

The learned bounds of a pair depend on the bounds of other state-action pairs.
In particular, whenever any bound is changed, we may need to re-learn the values for all other state-action pairs.
This is taken care of by globally resetting the learn flags to
yes
in
Algorithms

4
and
4
.
We highlight that this is one of the main differences to
[
10
]
, where samples are instead used to learn bounds on the transition probabilities while the actual values are propagated according to these estimates, trading memory for speed of convergence.

The need for the intermediate value
once
of
learn
arises from the asynchronicity of the updates.
Suppose an update of some pair
(s,a)
succeeds and we reset all learn values to
yes
.
However, for some other state-action pair
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
we are very close to an update, too.
Then, the values which will be used for an attempted update of
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
were mostly learned before the update of
(s,a)
.
Now, if for example
s
is a successor of
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
, the values of
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
may be influenced significantly by the update of
(s,a)
.
Hence, we need to learn the value of
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

once
more in order to be on the safe side.
A different solution approach would be to simply reset all
count
and
acc
values after every successful update, however this would be much less efficient:
If we again consider the above example, it might be the case that the values we gathered for
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
before the update of
(s,a)
already are sufficient for a successful update, discarding them would slow down convergence drastically.

In the algorithms of
[
129
,
33
]
, this problem instead is taken care of by remembering the last globally successful update.
There,
\texttt{learn}(s,a)
is only set to
no
if the previous attempted update of
(s,a)
happened after the last successful update.
This similarly implies that all values which are considered in the current update attempt are
“up to date”
.
We decided for this alternative approach since we have to track less variables.

5.3
Proof of Correctness

We now prove that
Algorithm

4
is probably approximately correct.
We first prove correctness of the result by showing that the computed bounds are faithful upper and lower bounds in
Lemma

5.9
.
However, we cannot guarantee that this is always the case due to statistical outliers.
Thus we first obtain bounds on the probability of these outliers.
Then, in order to prove termination with high probability, we argue that by our choice of constants the propagation of values is probably correct.
This means that whenever we update the bounds of a state-action pair
(s,a)
, the updated value is close to the true average under
\Delta(s,a)
.
Finally, we show that with high probability an update will occur as long as the bounds are not
\varepsilon
-close.

Lemma 5.3
.

The number of successful updates of
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
is bounded by
\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
each.

Proof 5.4
.

Let
a\in Act
be some action and
s=\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M})
the associated state.
The upper bound of
(s,a)
is initialised to
1
or
0
, similar for the lower bound.
Whenever
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
is updated in
Algorithm

4
, its value is decreased by at least
{\overline{\varepsilon}}
:
We have that
\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s,a)/m<\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)-2{\overline{\varepsilon}}
, hence
\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s,a)/m+{\overline{\varepsilon}}<\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)-{\overline{\varepsilon}}
.
Thus,
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)<\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)-{\overline{\varepsilon}}
.
Analogously,
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
is always increased by at least
{\overline{\varepsilon}}
whenever updated.

Moreover,
\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s,a)\geq 0
and
\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}(s,a)\leq{\overline{m}}
by initialization and update of these values, hence we never set
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
to a negative value and
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
is always smaller or equal to
1
.
Consequently, we change the value of
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
and
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
at most
\frac{1}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
times and there are at most
\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
successful updates to the upper and lower bounds, respectively.
Note that we do not necessarily have
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
for all executions of the algorithm, hence there are at most
\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
updates for each of the bounds individually.

Observe that this implies that for every execution, eventually there will be no more successful updates of
\mathsf{Up}
and the sampling strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
does not change.
This fact will be used in some of the subsequent proofs.
Moreover, we can use the above result to show that similarly, the number of
attempted
updates is bounded.

Lemma 5.5
.

The number of attempted updates of the upper bounds
\mathsf{Up}
and lower bounds
\mathsf{Lo}
is bounded by
{\overline{\xi}}=2\lvert Act\rvert(1+\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}})
, respectively.

Proof 5.6
.

Let
(s,a)\in S\times{Av}
be a state-action pair.
Suppose an update of
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
is attempted at step
\mathsf{t}
, i.e.
a_{\mathsf{t}}=a
,
\texttt{count}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)={\overline{m}}-1
, and
\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s,a)\neq\texttt{no}
.
Then, either the update is successful or
\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)
is updated with
Decrease
.
The learn flag is only set to
yes
again if some other upper bound is successfully updated.
Analogous reasoning applies to updates of the lower bounds.

By
Lemma

5.3
, there are at most
\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
successful updates to either bounds in total.
If an update of a particular state-action pair is attempted, it either succeeds or fails.
In the latter case, at most one more update of this state-action pair will be attempted until an other update succeeds.
Hence, for a particular state-action pair
(s,a)
we have in the worst case two attempted
\mathsf{Up}
-updates after every successful
\mathsf{Up}
-update (of
any
pair).
Together, there are at most
2+2\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
(two more attempts can occur after the last successful update).
Since there are
\lvert Act\rvert
state-action pairs in total, the statement follows.

Assumption 7
.

Suppose an
\mathsf{Up}
-update of the state-action pair
(s,a)
is attempted at step
\mathsf{t}
.
Let
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{\overline{m}}=\mathsf{t}
be the steps of the
{\overline{m}}
most recent visits to
(s,a)
.
Then
\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\val(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})\geq\val(s,a)-{\overline{\varepsilon}}
.
Analogously, for an attempted
\mathsf{Lo}
-update, we have
\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\val(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})\leq\val(s,a)+{\overline{\varepsilon}}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

The probability that
7
is violated during the execution of
Algorithm

4
is bounded by
\frac{\delta}{4}
.

Proof 5.8
.

We show that the claim for the upper bound is violated with probability at most
\frac{\delta}{8}
.
The lower bound part follows analogously and the overall claim via union bound.

Let
(s,a)
and
k_{i}
as in
7
, i.e. an
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
is attempted at step
k_{m}=\mathsf{t}
.
First, observe that due to the Markov property, the successor state under
(s,a)
does not depend on the algorithm’s execution.
Hence, the states
s_{k_{i}}^{\prime}
, i.e. the successor states after each visit of
(s,a)
, are distributed i.i.d. according to the underlying probability distribution
\Delta(s,a)
.
Define
Y_{i}=\val(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})
.
Clearly,
Y_{i}
are i.i.d., since the actual value of a state
\val(s)
is independent of the algorithm’s execution.
Moreover,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]=\val(s,a)
, since
\val
satisfies the fixed point conditions
\val(s,a)=\Delta(s,a)\langle\val\rangle
.
Define the empirical average
\underline{Y}=\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}Y_{i}
.
Observe that
\mathbb{E}[\underline{Y}]=\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]=\val(s,a)
.
By the Hoeffding bound
[
85
]
we have that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\underline{Y}\right]-\underline{Y}>{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right]\leq e^{-2{\overline{m}}{\overline{\varepsilon}}^{2}}=\frac{\delta}{8}\cdot{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}

By reordering, we obtain that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}[\val(s,a)-{\overline{\varepsilon}}>\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\val(s_{i})]\leq\frac{\delta}{8}\cdot{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}

[Fact I]
.
To conclude the proof, we extend the above argument to all steps
k_{1}
satisfying the preconditions of the assumption.
By
Lemma

5.5
, the number of attempted updates to
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
is bounded by
{\overline{\xi}}
, respectively
[Fact II]
.
Consequently, by employing the union bound, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}\left[\text{\ltxml@oqmark@open\textquotedblleft\penalty\hskip-0.0002pt\hskip 0.0002pt$\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\val(s_{k_{i}})<\val(s,a)-{\overline{\varepsilon}}$ for some $k_{1}$\textquotedblright\ltxml@oqmark@close{}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}\left[{\bigcup}_{k_{1}}\text{\ltxml@oqmark@open\textquotedblleft\penalty\hskip-0.0002pt\hskip 0.0002pt$\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\val(s_{k_{i}})<\val(s,a)-{\overline{\varepsilon}}$ for $k_{1}$\textquotedblright\ltxml@oqmark@close{}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql_no_ec:sampled_value_close_to_real_value_probability:bounded_probability}}}{\leq}~{\sum}_{k_{1}}\frac{\delta}{8}\cdot{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}~\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql_no_ec:sampled_value_close_to_real_value_probability:bounded_number}}}{\leq}~\frac{\delta}{8}.

Lemma 5.9
.

Assume that
7
holds.
Then, during any execution of
Algorithm

4
we have for every step
\mathsf{t}
, all states
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
and action
a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
that

\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)\leq\val(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a).

Proof 5.10
.

First, by definition of the algorithm we clearly have that
\mathsf{Up}
can only decrease and
\mathsf{Lo}
can only increase.
It remains to show that
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)\leq\val(s,a)\leq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
.
We proceed by induction on the step
\mathsf{t}
.
For
\mathsf{t}=0
, the statement clearly holds, since
\mathsf{Up}_{1}(s,a)=1
for all states except the special state
s_{-}
, which by assumption cannot reach the target
s_{+}
.
Analogously, the statement holds for
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}(s,a)
.
Now, fix an arbitrary step
\mathsf{t}
.
We have that
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}(s,a)\geq\val(s,a)
for all steps
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{t}

(IH)
.
Assume that
(s,a)
is the state-action pair sampled at step
\mathsf{t}
.
If no successful update takes place there is nothing to prove, since the values of
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
do not change.
Otherwise,
7
is applicable and we get

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)=\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathsf{Up}_{k_{i}}(s_{k_{i}})+{\overline{\varepsilon}}\overset{\ref{proof:dql_no_ec:bounds_ordered:ih}}{\geq}\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\val(s_{k_{i}})+{\overline{\varepsilon}}\geq\val(s,a).

Analogously, we have
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)\leq\val(s,a)
.

This gives us correctness of the returned result with high confidence upon termination.
It remains to show that the algorithm also terminates with high probability.

To this end, we introduce the upper bound maximizing strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
which selects in each state
s
uniformly among all actions maximal with respect to the
current
upper bounds, i.e.
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,\cdot)
.
This allows us to reason about the current value at step
\mathsf{t}
.
Note that this strategy differs from the sampling strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
, since
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
might change during an episode.
However, once there are no updates to upper bounds, we have that
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}=\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
.
We use this fact in the final convergence proof.
Once the two strategies align, we can transfer properties proven with respect to
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
to the actual sampling behaviour of the algorithm.

Using this strategy, we define the set of converged state-action pairs.

Definition 5.11
.

For every step
\mathsf{t}
, define
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}\subseteq S\times{Av}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(s,a)\mid\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)-\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}]\rangle\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\}\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(s,a)\mid\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}]\rangle-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\},

i.e. all state-action pairs whose
\mathsf{Up}
- or
\mathsf{Lo}
-value is close to the respective value of its successors under
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
.
If
(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
, we say that
(s,a)
is
\mathsf{Up}
-converged at step
\mathsf{t}
, analogously
(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}
is called
\mathsf{Lo}
-converged at step
\mathsf{t}
.

The approach for the convergence proof is to show that (with high probability) (i) if an update of some bound fails, the current bound is consistent with its successors, i.e. the respective pair is converged, and (ii) we visit non-converged pairs only finitely often.
Finally, we combine these two facts non-trivially to prove convergence.

Lemma 5.12
.

We have for every step
\mathsf{t}
and state
s
that

\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand \hskip 10.22217pt}\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s).

Moreover, if
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
, then
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
for all
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}>\mathsf{t}
until an
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
succeeds.
If no more updates of upper bounds take place, the analogous statement holds for lower bounds, too.

Proof 5.13
.

Since the strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
maximizes the upper bound we have

\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)={\sum}_{a\in{Av}(s)}\pi_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)\cdot\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)={\max}_{a\in{Av}(s)}\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s).

We also trivially have that
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
, as
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
is the maximum over all actions.

For the second claim, recall that
\mathsf{Up}
-values can only decrease.
If
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
, we have
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)>3{\overline{\varepsilon}}+\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}]\rangle=3{\overline{\varepsilon}}+\Delta(s,a)\langle\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle
.
Since (i)
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)
unless a successful
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
occurs and (ii)
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)\geq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s)
for all states
s
, we obtain the claim.
The lower bound statement is proven analogously, noting that once upper bounds remain fixed the only way to change
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}
is a successful update of some lower bound.

Assumption 8
.

Suppose an update of the upper bound (lower bound) of the state-action pair
(s,a)
is attempted at step
\mathsf{t}
.
Let
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{\overline{m}}=\mathsf{t}
be the steps of the
{\overline{m}}
most recent visits to
(s,a)
.
If
(s,a)
is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged (
\mathsf{Lo}
-converged) at step
k_{1}
, the update at step
\mathsf{t}
is successful.

Intuitively, this assumption says that whenever the bound for a state-action pair is significantly different from its successors and we visit that pair often enough, we obtain a significantly better estimate.
We cannot guarantee this surely due to outliers, but we bound the probability of this assumption being violated using our choice of the delay
{\overline{m}}
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The probability that
8
is violated during the execution of
Algorithm

4
is bounded by
\frac{\delta}{4}
.

Proof 5.15
.

As in
Lemma

5.7
, we prove that an attempted update of the upper bounds fails with probability at most
\frac{\delta}{8}
.
The same bound then can be obtained for the lower bound variant with a mostly analogous proof.
The overall result again follows using the union bound.

Let
(s,a)
and
k_{i}
as in
8
, i.e.
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{k_{1}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
and an update of the upper bound is attempted at step
\mathsf{t}

[Fact I]
.
Define
X_{i}=\pi_{k_{1}}[\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}](s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})
.
Note that all
X_{i}
are defined using
\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}
and
\pi_{k_{1}}
(instead of
\mathsf{Up}_{k_{i}}
and
\pi_{k_{i}}
).
Consequently, the
X_{i}
are i.i.d. and we can apply the Hoeffding bound to the empirical average
\underline{X}=\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}X_{i}
.
This yields that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}[\underline{X}-\mathbb{E}[\underline{X}]\geq{\overline{\varepsilon}}]\leq e^{-2{\overline{m}}{\overline{\varepsilon}}^{2}}=\frac{\delta}{8}\cdot{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}.

Since the
X_{i}
are i.i.d., we have that
\mathbb{E}[\underline{X}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]
for all
1\leq i\leq{\overline{m}}
, in particular
\mathbb{E}[\underline{X}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]
.
Thus, the probability that
\underline{X}-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]\geq{\overline{\varepsilon}}
is at most
\frac{\delta}{8}\cdot{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}

[Fact II]
.
For the lower bound proof, we analogously define
X_{i}=\pi_{k_{1}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{1}}](s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})
and prove that
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]-\underline{X}\geq{\overline{\varepsilon}}
with the same probability.

Now, we show that if
\underline{X}-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]<{\overline{\varepsilon}}
the update at step
\mathsf{t}
will be successful
[Fact III]
.
Recall that an update is successful when the
{\overline{m}}
most recent samples significantly differ from the currently stored value, i.e. when the currently stored value
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
is significantly larger than the newly learned value.
We have that (reasoning below)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)-\frac{1}{m}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathsf{Up}_{k_{i}}(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)-\frac{1}{m}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})

(2)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)-\frac{1}{m}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\pi_{k_{1}}[\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}](s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})

(3)

\displaystyle\qquad>\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]-{\overline{\varepsilon}}

(4)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}(s,a)-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]-{\overline{\varepsilon}}

(5)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}(s,a)-\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi_{k_{1}}[\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}]\rangle-{\overline{\varepsilon}}

(6)

\displaystyle\qquad>2{\overline{\varepsilon}}.

(7)

Inequality (
2
) follows from the fact that
\mathsf{Up}
-values can only decrease over time by definition of the algorithm.
Equality (
3
) follows directly from
Lemma

5.12
.
Inequality (
4
) follows from the above derivation.
Equality (
5
) follows from the fact that
\mathsf{Up}_{k_{i}}(s,a)=\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}(s,a)
for all
1\leq i\leq{\overline{m}}
:
Since an update is attempted at step
k_{\overline{m}}=t
, there can be no update attempts in the previous
{\overline{m}}-1
visits, consequently the value of
\mathsf{Up}_{k_{i}}(s,a)
does not change between
k_{1}
and
k_{\overline{m}}
.
Equality (
6
) follows directly from the definition of
X_{1}
.
Finally, Inequality (
7
) follows from
5.15
, i.e. that
(s,a)
is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged at step
k_{1}
, formally
\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}(s,a)-\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi_{k_{1}}[\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}]\rangle>3{\overline{\varepsilon}}
.

For the lower bound, we prove a similar result:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{i}}(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)\geq\frac{1}{m}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{1}}(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\frac{1}{m}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\pi_{k_{1}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{1}}](s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\qquad>\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]-{\overline{\varepsilon}}-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]-{\overline{\varepsilon}}-\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{1}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\qquad=\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi_{k_{1}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{1}}]\rangle-{\overline{\varepsilon}}-\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{1}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\qquad>2{\overline{\varepsilon}}.

The only major difference lies in the second inequality (corresponding to Equality (
3
)), where we instead use the fact that
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
.

Finally, we again extend the argument to all steps
k_{1}
similar to
Lemma

5.7
, i.e. that by
Lemma

5.5
the number of attempted updates is bounded by
{\overline{\xi}}

[Fact IV]
.
Together with the union bound, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}\left[\text{\ltxml@oqmark@open\textquotedblleft\penalty\hskip-0.0002pt\hskip 0.0002pt\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{asm:dql_no_ec:converged_successful_update} is violated for $\mathsf{Up}$\textquotedblright\ltxml@oqmark@close{}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=~\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}\left[{\bigcup}_{k_{1}}\text{\ltxml@oqmark@open\textquotedblleft\penalty\hskip-0.0002pt\hskip 0.0002pt$k_{1}$ satisfies condition \ref{proof:dql_no_ec:converged_successful_update_probability:attempt}, but the $\mathsf{Up}$-update fails\textquotedblright\ltxml@oqmark@close{}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq~{\sum}_{k_{1}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}\left[\text{\ltxml@oqmark@open\textquotedblleft\penalty\hskip-0.0002pt\hskip 0.0002pt$k_{1}$ satisfies condition \ref{proof:dql_no_ec:converged_successful_update_probability:attempt}, but the $\mathsf{Up}$-update fails\textquotedblright\ltxml@oqmark@close{}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql_no_ec:converged_successful_update_probability:update_success}}}{\leq}~{\sum}_{k_{1}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}}\left[\text{\ltxml@oqmark@open\textquotedblleft\penalty\hskip-0.0002pt\hskip 0.0002pt$\underline{X}-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]\geq{\overline{\varepsilon}}$ for $k_{1}$\textquotedblright\ltxml@oqmark@close{}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql_no_ec:converged_successful_update_probability:difference_bound}}}{\leq}~{\sum}_{k_{1}}\frac{\delta}{8}\cdot{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}~\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql_no_ec:converged_successful_update_probability:bounded_number}}}{\leq}~\frac{\delta}{8}.

Lemma 5.16
.

Assume that
8
holds.
If an attempted
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
at step
\mathsf{t}
fails and
\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)=\texttt{no}
, then
(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.
If no more updates of upper bounds take place, the analogous statement holds for the lower bounds, too.

Proof 5.17
.

We prove the statement for the upper bound, with the corresponding lower bound statement following analogously.
Assume an unsuccessful
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
occurs at step
\mathsf{t}
and let
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{\overline{m}}=\mathsf{t}
be the
{\overline{m}}
most recent visits to
(s,a)
.
We consider three cases:

1.

If
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{k_{1}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
, then by
8
the
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
at step
\mathsf{t}
will be successful and there is nothing to prove.

2.

We have
(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}_{k_{1}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
and there exists
i\in\{2,\ldots,{\overline{m}}\}
such that
(s,a)
is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged at step
k_{i}
.
It follows that there must have been a successful update of some
\mathsf{Up}
-value between steps
k_{1}
and
k_{\overline{m}}
, say step
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
.
By
Algorithm

4
,
\texttt{learn}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}(s,a)
is set to
yes
and there is nothing to prove.

3.

For the last case, we have that for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\overline{m}}\}
that
(s,a)
is
\mathsf{Up}
-converged at step
k_{i}
, particularly
(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}_{k_{{\overline{m}}}}^{\mathsf{Up}}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.
As the attempt to update the
\mathsf{Up}
-value of
(s,a)
at step
\mathsf{t}
was unsuccessful, we have that
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.

For the lower bound statement, observe that
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}
may be changed by a successful update of
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}
.
Hence, the above reasoning can only be followed once upper bounds do not change.

Lemma 5.18
.

Assume that
8
holds.
Then, there are at most
2{\overline{m}}\cdot\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
visits to state-action pairs which are not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged.
Moreover, once the upper bounds are not updated any more, there are at most
2{\overline{m}}\cdot\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
visits to state-action pairs which are not
\mathsf{Lo}
-converged.

Proof 5.19
.

We show that whenever a state-action pair
(s,a)
is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged at step
\mathsf{t}
, then in at most
2{\overline{m}}
more visits to
(s,a)
a successful
\mathsf{Up}
-update will occur.
Assume that
(s,a)
is visited at step
\mathsf{t}
and it is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged, i.e.
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.
We distinguish two cases.

1.

\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)=\texttt{no}
:
This implies that the last attempted
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
was not successful.
Let
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
be the step of this attempt,
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}<\mathsf{t}
.
We have
\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+1}(s,a)=\texttt{no}
.
By
Lemma

5.16
, we have that
(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.
Since we assumed
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
, there was a successful update of some
\mathsf{Up}
-value between
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{t}
, otherwise we would have
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.
Consequently, we have
\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(s,a)=\texttt{yes}
.
By
8
the next attempted
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
will be successful.
This attempt will occur after
{\overline{m}}
more visits to
(s,a)
.

2.

\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)\neq\texttt{no}
:
By construction of the algorithm, we have that in at most
{\overline{m}}-1
more visits to
(s,a)
, an
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
will be attempted.
Suppose this attempt takes place at step
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\geq\mathsf{t}
and the most
{\overline{m}}
recent visits to
