(s,a)
prior to
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
happened at steps
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{\overline{m}}=\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
.
Note that we do not necessarily have that
\mathsf{t}=k_{1}
or
\mathsf{t}=k_{\overline{m}}
, but surely
\mathsf{t}\in\{k_{1},\dots,k_{\overline{m}}\}
.
If the
\mathsf{Up}
-update at step
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
succeeds, there is nothing to prove, hence assume that this update fails.
There are two possibilities:

(a)

If
(s,a)
is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged at step
k_{1}
, then by
8
the
\mathsf{Up}
-update at step
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
will be successful, contradicting the assumption.

(b)

If instead
(s,a)
is
\mathsf{Up}
-converged at step
k_{1}
, we have that
\mathcal{K}_{k_{1}}^{\mathsf{Up}}\neq\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
, since we assumed that
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.
Consequently, there was a successful
\mathsf{Up}
-update of some other state-action pair at some step
\mathsf{t}^{\prime\prime}
with
k_{1}<\mathsf{t}^{\prime\prime}\leq\mathsf{t}
and thus
\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime\prime}+1}(s,a)=\texttt{yes}
.
Moreover, we necessarily have that no
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
is attempted after
\mathsf{t}^{\prime\prime}
.
Together, we have that
\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+1}(s,a)=\texttt{once}
even though the attempted
\mathsf{Up}
-update at step
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
fails.
By
Lemma

5.12
, we have that
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}
, as
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
and no successful
\mathsf{Up}
-update of
(s,a)
occurred between
\mathsf{t}
and
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
.
By
8
the next attempt to update
\mathsf{Up}
-value of
(s,a)
will succeed.

By
Lemma

5.3
, the number of successful
\mathsf{Up}
-updates is bounded by
\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
, and by the previous arguments we have that if for some
\mathsf{t}
the pair
(s,a)
is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged then in at most
2{\overline{m}}
more visits to
(s,a)
, there will be a successful update to
\mathsf{Up}(s,a)
.
Hence, there can be at most
2{\overline{m}}\cdot\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
steps
\mathsf{t}
such that the current state-action pair is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged.
Once no more
\mathsf{Up}
-updates take place,
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
remains fixed and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}
only changes due to successful updates of the lower bounds, yielding an analogous proof for
\mathsf{Lo}
.

As a last auxiliary lemma, we show that whenever the probability of reaching a non-converged pair is low, we necessarily are close to the optimal value.

Lemma 5.20
.

Assume that
7
holds and fix a step
\mathsf{t}
.
Then, for every state
s\in S
we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]\leq

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\leq

\displaystyle\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)+3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}].

Proof 5.21
.

The central idea of this proof is to apply
Lemma

A.9
twice, with
X(s,a)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
and
X(s,a)=\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s,a)
, respectively.

For the first application, set
\kappa_{l}=-1
,
\kappa_{u}=3{\overline{\varepsilon}}
, and
\pi=\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
.
Then,
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
and

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]

(8)

since
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
are equivalent on
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.
The lemma then yields that

\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{t},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}.

(9)

Recall that
\pi_{t}
is a strategy randomizing uniformly over some of the available actions in each state, hence
\delta_{\min}(\pi)
is at least
p_{\min}
.
For the second application, we dually set
\kappa_{l}=-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}
,
\kappa_{u}=1
, and
\pi=\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
.
Again, we have
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}
and

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}].

(10)

The lemma gives us

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{t},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]-\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}.

(11)

Now, recall that
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
and
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)

[Fact I]
due to
Lemma

5.12
.
Together, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}\overset{\ref{proof:dql_no_ec:bounds_close:expected_strat}}{=}\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}\overset{\eqref{eq:stm:dql_no_ec:bounds_close:up_strat}}{\leq}\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{t},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}],

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]\overset{\eqref{eq:stm:dql_no_ec:bounds_close:up_probs}}{\leq}\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\overset{\eqref{eq:stm:dql_no_ec:bounds_close:low_probs}}{\leq}\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}],\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{t},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\overset{\eqref{eq:stm:dql_no_ec:bounds_close:low_strat}}{\leq}3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}+\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)\overset{\ref{proof:dql_no_ec:bounds_close:expected_strat}}{\leq}3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}+\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s).

Combining all the above statements now yields the overall result.

Theorem 5.22
.

Algorithm

4
terminates and yields a correct result with probability at least
1-\delta
after at most
\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{POLY}(\lvert Act\rvert,p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert},\varepsilon^{-1},\ln\delta))
steps.

Proof 5.23
.

We only consider executions where
7
and
8
hold.
By
Lemmas

5.7
and
5.14
together with the union bound, this happens with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{2}
.

Now, observe that if the algorithm terminates at some step
\mathsf{t}
, we have that
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})<\varepsilon
by definition.
With
Lemma

5.9
, we get
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})\leq\val(\hat{s})\leq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})
.
Reordering yields the result.

We show by contradiction that the algorithm terminates for almost all considered executions.
Thus, assume that the execution does not halt with non-zero probability.
Since the MDP
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
1
, almost all episodes eventually visit either
s_{+}
or
s_{-}
due to
Lemma

2.9
and thus are of finite length.
Thus, almost all executions for which the algorithm does not terminate comprise infinitely many episodes.
We restrict our attention to only those executions.

Recall that due to
Lemma

5.5
, there are only finitely many attempted updates on almost all considered executions.
Consequently, on these executions the algorithm eventually does not change
\mathsf{Up}
, since no successful updates can occur from some step
\mathsf{t}
onwards.
This means that all following samples are obtained by sampling according to the strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
.
Note that both the time of convergence and the actual strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
depends on the execution
\mathfrak{a}
.
Thus, we need to employ
Lemma

A.13
—the algorithm clearly qualifies as Markov process, since its evolution only depends on its current valuations.
More precisely, it is not difficult to see that the whole execution of the algorithm (with fixed inputs) can be modelled as a (very unwieldy) countable Markov chain, showing that the considered properties are measurable.
In particular, they are reachability objectives on this induced Markov chain.

Let us now consider the set of executions for which the upper bounds eventually converge and moreover
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]\geq\rho>0
infinitely often.
Assume that this set of executions has a non-zero measure.
By
Lemma

A.13
, on almost all of these executions
\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}
is also reached infinitely often, contradicting
Lemma

5.18
.
For the lower bounds, we can prove a completely analogous statement.
Consequently,
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]\to 0
and
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}]\to 0
on almost all considered executions for
\mathsf{t}\to\infty
.

Inserting the definition of
{\overline{\varepsilon}}
, we have for a sufficiently large step
\mathsf{t}
that

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]

and dually

\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})+3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}]<\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

for all considered executions.
Thus, by
Lemma

5.20
, we have

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]<\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

i.e.
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})<\varepsilon
, contradicting the assumption.

We have proven that the result is approximately correct with probability
1-\frac{\delta}{2}
.
Now, we additionally need to prove the step bound.
To this end, we first bound the number of sampled paths and then bound the length of each path.
Central to the following proof is
Lemma

5.18
, bounding the number of visits to non-converged state-action pairs.
First, we treat the upper bounds.
Observe that the probability of visiting a non-
\mathsf{Up}
-converged state-action pair either is
0
or at least
p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
(due to
Lemma

A.1
).
Moreover, while this probability may fluctuate, once it reaches
0
it remains at
0
, since then the sampling strategy does not change and all pairs reachable under this strategy are
\mathsf{Up}
-converged.
So, in the worst case, the probability of reaching such a pair is exactly
p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
until they are visited often enough.
We model this process as a series of Bernoulli trials
X_{i}
, equalling
1
if at least one
\mathsf{Up}
-update happens while sampling the
i
-th path.
10
10

10

We deliberately use
i
instead of
\mathsf{e}
to emphasize that
X_{i}
does not operate on the probability space of the algorithm
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{A}})
.
Instead, they represent a crude under-approximation to allow for a feasible analysis.

While the exact probabilities are not independent, they are always at least as large as the success probability
p\coloneqq p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
of these trials (or
0
if all reachable pairs are
\mathsf{Up}
-converged).
Hence, we approximate the number of trials we need to perform until we observe at least
c\coloneqq 2{\overline{m}}\cdot\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
successes with high probability—then, all upper bounds necessarily are converged by
Lemma

5.18
.
Now, we are essentially dealing with a binomially distributed variable
X_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
and want to find an
n
such that
\mathbb{P}[X_{n}\geq c]\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{4}
.
Since we are interested in the limit behaviour, we can apply the de Moivre–Laplace theorem, allowing us to replace this binomial distribution with an appropriate normal distribution.
Thus, we obtain

\mathbb{P}[X_{n}\geq c]\approx 1-\Phi\left(\frac{c-np}{\sqrt{np(1-p)}}\right),

and rearranging yields

n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(c-np)\approx\Phi^{-1}\left(\frac{\delta}{4}\right)\cdot\sqrt{p(1-p)}.

For readability, we set
a\coloneqq\Phi^{-1}\left(\frac{\delta}{4}\right)
.
Solving for
n
gives us

n\approx\frac{c}{p}-\frac{a}{2p}\sqrt{(1-p)^{2}a^{2}+4c(1-p)}+\frac{(1-p)r^{2}}{2p}.

Inserting the definitions yields that
n\in\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{POLY}(\lvert Act\rvert,p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert},\varepsilon^{-1},\ln\delta))
.
This bounds the number of paths sampled by the algorithm.
We furthermore prove that the length of all those paths is polynomial with high probability.
To this end, we employ
Lemma

A.5
.
Recall that sampling of a path stops once we reach one of the two special states
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
.
Due to
1
, the probability of eventually reaching them is
1
.
Hence,
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond^{\leq N}\{s_{+},s_{-}\}]\geq 1-\tau
, where
N\geq\ln(\frac{2}{\tau})\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}
for any sampling strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
.
In other words, the probability of a sampled path being longer than
N
is at most
\tau
.
Then, by the union bound, the probability of any of the
n
paths being longer than
N
is at most
n\cdot\tau
.
By choosing
\tau=\frac{\delta}{4n}
, this happens with probability at most
\frac{\delta}{4}
.
Then,
\ln(\frac{2}{\tau})=\ln(8n)-\ln(\delta)
, i.e. the length of each path again is bounded by a polynomial in the input values.
Together, we obtain the results, since polynomials are closed under multiplication.

Remark 5.24

(Relation to
[
129
]
)
.

Before we proceed to the general case, we briefly discuss how our proof structure relates to the one of
[
129
]
and how it can be used to derive a variant of their Theorem 1.
Most of our proofs are quite analogous.
For example,
8
is practically equivalent to their Assumption A1, similarly
Lemmas

5.14
,
5.9
,
5.16
and
5.18
and the respective proofs correspond to their Lemma 1 to 4 (however, note the different bounds).
Since we are dealing with unbounded reachability (assuming almost sure absorption by
1
), the purpose of Lemma 5 corresponds to our
Lemma

A.9
.

Major differences arise in the actual proof of
[
129
, Theorem 1]
.
As we already pointed out, the Hoeffding bound is not applicable to variables indicating whether an update has occurred in a particular step due to the clear dependency.
The related proof step aims to show that with high probability after a certain number of steps, the number of possible updates is exhausted (by virtue of
Lemma

5.18
) and then bounds the deviation from the true value based on this.
We prove a similar statement via
Lemma

5.20
, connecting the probability of visiting a non-converged state-action pair to the convergence of the bounds.
Note that the proof of
Lemma

5.20
employs the auxiliary
Lemma

A.9
.

6
Limited Information – General Case

As before, MECs pose an additional challenge, since they introduce superfluous upper fixed points.
The key difference to the full information setting is that MECs cannot be directly identified.
Instead, we identify a set of state-action pairs as an end component if it occurs sufficiently often.
By bounding the probability of falsely identifying such a set as an end component, we can replicate the previous proof structure.

6.1
Collapsing End Components with Limited Information

Before we present the complete algorithm, we first show how we identify end components in this section.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,Act,{Av},\Delta)
be an MDP,
\rho\in\mathsf{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}}
and
i,j\geq 0
.
We define the state-action pairs which appear at least
i
times on the path
\rho
during the first
j
steps as

Appear(\rho,i,j)=\{(s,a)\in S\times{Av}\mid\lvert\{k\mid k\leq j\land\rho^{a}(k)=a\}\rvert\geq i\}.

We overload the definition of
Appear
to also accept finite paths of sufficient length.
Moreover, we also define
Appear
for paths of Markov chains, which yields the states occurring more than
i
times.

For notational convenience, we identify the result of
Appear
with the corresponding state-action tuple
(R,B)
since we will use these results as candidates for end components.
With appropriate
i
and
j
,
Appear
is an EC with high probability.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,Act,{Av},\Delta)
be an MDP,
\hat{s}\in S
an initial state,
T\subseteq S
a set of target states, and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{MD}}
a memoryless strategy on
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond\overline{T}]=0
for all
s\in T
, i.e.
T
is absorbing under
\pi
.
Set
S_{\pi}=\bigcup_{s\in S}\support(\pi(s))
,
\kappa=\lvert S_{\pi}\rvert+1
, and pick
i\geq\kappa
.
Then either
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi}[\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T]=1
or

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi}\!\left[{App}_{i}\mid\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!\geq 1-2(1+i^{2})\cdot e^{-(i-1)\frac{\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\kappa}}{\kappa}}\cdot\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{-\kappa},

where
{App}_{i}=\{\rho\in\mathsf{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}}\mid Appear(\rho,i,2i^{3})\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M})\}
.

Informally, this lemma shows that, when sampling according to a memoryless strategy, paths of sufficient length either end up in an already known set of ECs or frequently reappearing state-action pairs also form an EC with high probability.

Proof 6.3
.

If
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi}[\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T]=1
, there is nothing to prove, hence we assume the opposite, i.e. that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},\hat{s}}^{\pi}[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}]>0

[Fact I]
.

Given an MDP, a designated initial state
\hat{s}
, and a memoryless strategy, we can construct a finite state Markov chain which exactly captures the behaviour of the MDP under the given strategy.
We define the Markov chain
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}=(\{\hat{s}\}\mathbin{\cup}S_{\pi},\delta_{\pi})
, where
\delta_{\pi}
is defined as

\displaystyle\delta(\hat{s},a)

\displaystyle=\pi(\hat{s},a)\text{ for $a\in\support(\pi(\hat{s}))$}

\displaystyle\delta(a,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Delta(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}),a,\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{\prime},\mathcal{M}))\cdot\pi(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a^{\prime},\mathcal{M}),a^{\prime}).

In other words,
\delta(a,a^{\prime})
equals the probability of reaching some state
s^{\prime}
after playing action
a
and then continuing with action
a^{\prime}
.
Recall that each action is tied to a unique state.
As such, the paths in
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
exactly correspond to the paths in
\mathcal{M}
following
\pi
.
Furthermore, it is easy to see that each BSCC of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
corresponds to an end component in
\mathcal{M}
.
Observe that, by definition,
\kappa
equals the number of states in
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}

[Fact II]
and
\delta_{\min}(\pi)
equals the smallest positive transition probability in
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}

[Fact III]
.
For readability, we define
c=\exp(-\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\kappa}/\kappa)
.

Let
{App}_{i,\pi}\subseteq\mathsf{Paths}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi}}
be the event corresponding to
{App}_{i}
in the Markov chain
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
.
Informally,
{App}_{i,\pi}
denotes the set of all (infinite) paths
\rho
which within
2i^{3}
steps (i) visit all states of some BSCC at least
i
times, and (ii) all other states at most
i-1
times, i.e. all paths such that
Appear(\rho,i,2i^{3})
is a BSCC of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
.
We now show that

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}[{App}_{i,\pi}\mid\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}]\geq 1-2c^{i}i^{3}\cdot\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{-\kappa},

i.e. the probability of
{App}_{i,\pi}
given that
T
is not reached within
2i^{3}
steps is at least
1-2c^{i}i^{3}\cdot\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{-\kappa}
.
Since the paths of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
exactly correspond to paths obtained in
\mathcal{M}
by following the strategy
\pi
, this proves the claim.

First, we show that
[Fact IV]

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[{App}_{i,\pi}\right]\!\geq 1-2(1+i^{2})\cdot c^{i-1}.

Let
B=\bigcup_{R\in\BsccsOp(\mathsf{M}_{\pi})}R
be the set of all states in BSCCs of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
.
We have that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}[\Diamond B]=1
by
Lemma

2.7
.
We apply
Lemma

A.5
with
N=i-1
and
\tau=2c^{i-1}
.
By
6.3
and
6.3

\lvert S_{\pi}\rvert\cdot\ln\left(\frac{2}{\tau}\right)\cdot\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{-\lvert S_{\pi}\rvert}=\kappa\cdot\ln\left(\exp\left((i-1)\cdot\frac{\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\kappa}}{\kappa}\right)\right)\cdot\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{-\kappa}=i-1.

Thus
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}[\Diamond^{\leq i-1}B]\geq 1-2c^{i-1}
.
In other words, an infinite path of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
starting in
\hat{s}
does not visit a BSCC of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
within
i-1
steps with probability at most
2c^{i-1}
.

Now, let
R=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}\subseteq B
be some BSCC of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
and fix two states
s_{i},s_{j}\in R
.
Since
R
is an BSCC, we have
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},s_{i}}[\Diamond\{s_{j}\}]=1
, and we can apply
Lemma

A.5
again to obtain that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},s_{i}}[\Diamond^{\leq i}\{s_{j}\}]\geq 1-2c^{i-1}
.
Consequently, the probability of visiting all states of
R
, one after another, with at most
i-1
steps between visiting the respective next state, is at least
1-n\cdot 2c^{i-1}
.
Repeating this argument, with probability at least
1-i\cdot n\cdot 2c^{i-1}\geq 1-i\cdot\kappa\cdot 2c^{i-1}
, this round trip is successful
i
times in a row and has a length of at most
i\cdot n\cdot(i-1)\leq i^{2}\kappa\leq i^{3}
.
Using the union bound again, we get that with probability at least
1-2c^{i-1}-i\kappa\cdot 2c^{i-1}=1-2c^{i-1}(1+i\kappa)\geq 1-2(1+i^{2})\cdot c^{i-1}
a path of length
i^{3}
ends up in a BSCC within
i-1
steps and then visits all states of the BSCC at least
i
times, proving
6.3
.

Let
T_{\pi}=\{a\in S_{\pi}\mid\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M})\in T\}
the states of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
corresponding to the given state set
T
.
Recall that we assumed that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond\overline{T}]=0
for
s\in T
, i.e.
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},a}[\Diamond\overline{T_{\pi}}]=0
for all
a\in T_{\pi}
(recall that the states of
\mathsf{M}
are actions
a
of
\mathcal{M}
).
Consequently, each BSCC of
\mathsf{M}_{\pi}
either is contained in
T_{\pi}
or disjoint from it:
Assume that there exists a BSCC
R
with states
a,a^{\prime}\in R
where
a\in T_{\pi}
and
a^{\prime}\notin T_{\pi}
.
Since
R
is a BSCC, we have
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},a}[\Diamond\{a^{\prime}\}]=1
, contradicting
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},a}[\Diamond\overline{T_{\pi}}]=0
.

Due to
6.3
, there exists at least one BSCC which is disjoint from
T_{\pi}
—otherwise any run would eventually end up in
T_{\pi}
.
Let
s
be some state in this BSCC.
By construction, there exists a path of length at most
\kappa
from
\hat{s}
to
s

6.3
, and thus the probability of reaching such a BSCC is bounded from below by
\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\kappa}
, using
6.3
.
Formally, we have
[Fact V]

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T_{\pi}}\right]\!>\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\kappa}.

Finally, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[{App}_{i,\pi}\mid\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!\overset{\ref{proof:dql:appear_is_ec_probability:assumption}}{~=~}\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[{App}_{i,\pi}\mathbin{\cap}\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!/\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!

\displaystyle\qquad~=~\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[{App}_{i,\pi}\setminus\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T\right]\!/\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!

\displaystyle\qquad~=~(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[{App}_{i,\pi}\right]\!-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[{App}_{i,\pi}\mathbin{\cap}\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T\right]\!)/\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!

\displaystyle\qquad~\geq~(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[{App}_{i,\pi}\right]\!-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T\right]\!)/\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql:appear_is_ec_probability:absolute_bound}}}{~\geq~}(1-2c^{i-1}(1+i^{2})-(1-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!))/\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!

\displaystyle\qquad~=~(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!-2c^{i-1}(1+i^{2}))/\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!

\displaystyle\qquad~=~1-(2c^{i-1}(1+i^{2}))/\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M}_{\pi},\hat{s}}\!\left[\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}T}\right]\!

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql:appear_is_ec_probability:not_reach_probability}}}{~\geq~}1-2(1+i^{2})\cdot c^{i-1}\cdot\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{-\kappa}.

6.2
The General DQL Algorithm

Input:

Inputs as given in
Definition

5.2
, precision
\varepsilon
, and confidence
\delta
.

Output:

Values
(l,u)
which are
\varepsilon
-optimal, i.e.,
\val(\hat{s})\in[l,u]
and
0\leq u-l<\varepsilon
, with probability at least
1-\delta
.

1

Initialize all variables as in
Algorithm

4

2

\mathsf{e}\leftarrow 1
,
\mathsf{t}\leftarrow 1

3

for

s\in S

do

\mathsf{collapsed}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\leftarrow s

4

S_{1}\leftarrow S
,
{Av}_{1}\leftarrow{Av}
,
T_{1}\leftarrow T
,
\mathsf{Z}_{1}\leftarrow\emptyset

5

6

while

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s})\geq\varepsilon

do

7

for

s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}

do

\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\leftarrow\argmax_{a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)}\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)

8

9

s_{\mathsf{t}}\leftarrow\hat{s}
,
\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{e}}\leftarrow\mathsf{t}

10

11

while

s_{\mathsf{t}}\notin T_{\mathsf{e}}\cup\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}

and

\mathsf{t}-\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{e}}<2\mathsf{i}^{3}

do

12

a_{\mathsf{t}}\leftarrow
sampled uniformly from
\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{\mathsf{t}})

//
Pick an action

s_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{succ}(a_{\mathsf{t}})

//
Query successor oracle

s_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime\prime})

13

Perform updates as in
Algorithm

4

//
Update Bounds

s_{\mathsf{t}+1}\leftarrow s_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{t}\leftarrow\mathsf{t}+1

14

15

if

\mathsf{t}-\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{e}}\geq 2\mathsf{i}^{3}

then

//
Update ECs

16

(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})\leftarrow Appear(s_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{e}}}a_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{e}}}s_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{e}}+1}\dots a_{\mathsf{t}-1}s_{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{i},2\mathsf{i}^{3})

17

C\leftarrow\bigcup_{s\in\mathsf{R}}{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\setminus B

18

19

if

\mathsf{B}\neq\emptyset

then

20

if

T_{\mathsf{e}}\cap\mathsf{R}\neq\emptyset

then

21

T_{\mathsf{e}+1}\leftarrow T_{\mathsf{e}}\cup\mathsf{R}

22

for

a\in\mathsf{B}

do

\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)\leftarrow 1

23

else if

C=\emptyset

then

24

\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}+1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}\cup\mathsf{R}

25

for

a\in\mathsf{B}

do

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)\leftarrow 0

26

else

27

S_{\mathsf{e}+1}\leftarrow(S_{\mathsf{e}}\setminus R)\cup\{s_{(R,B)}\}

28

{Av}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s_{(R,B)})\leftarrow C

29

for

s\in\mathsf{R}\cup\{s_{(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})}\}

do

\mathsf{collapsed}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s)\leftarrow s_{(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})}

30

if

\hat{s}\in\mathsf{R}

then

\hat{s}\leftarrow s_{(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})}

31

32

\mathsf{e}\leftarrow\mathsf{e}+1

33

return

(\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s}),\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\hat{s}))

34

Algorithm 5

The DQL learning algorithm for general MDPs.

We define the general DQL algorithm in
Algorithm

5
.
Essentially, the algorithm works similar to the previous
Algorithm

4
.
The main difference is that it further employs
Lemma

6.2
to detect whether the current sample is stuck in a yet to be discovered EC.
To this end, the algorithm introduces a small set of additional auxiliary variables, necessary to track representative states similar to the collapsed MDP of
Section

4
.
In particular,
\mathsf{collapsed}_{\mathsf{e}}
stores the representatives of each state.
Since we might discover growing ECs, this representative might be part of another already discovered EC.
Thus, we use
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}
to resolve the current representative of a given state
s
by repeatedly applying
\mathsf{collapsed}_{\mathsf{e}}
until a fixed point is reached.
Practically, we would store
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}
as a map, pointing each
“original”
state of the MDP to its current representative.
We introduce the
“layered”
representation through
\mathsf{collapsed}_{\mathsf{e}}
only as notational convenience.
Additionally,
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
contains all states which are part of a bottom EC without a target state.
We choose the parameter
\mathsf{i}
, controlling the length of each sample and when to check for an EC, such that

\lvert Act\rvert\cdot 2(1+\mathsf{i}^{2})\cdot e^{-(\mathsf{i}-1)\frac{p_{\min}(\pi)^{\lvert S\rvert+1}}{\lvert S\rvert+1}}\cdot p_{\min}(\pi)^{-(\lvert S\rvert+1)}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{i}\geq\lvert Act\rvert.

(12)

This technical choice becomes more apparent in the proof of
Lemma

6.21
.
Note that such an
\mathsf{i}
always exists since the left side of the first inequality converges to
0
for
\mathsf{i}\to\infty
.
Moreover, we can find such an
\mathsf{i}
using the values provided by the limited information setting as defined in
Definition

5.2
.

Remark 6.4
.

In contrast to the previous sections, the domain of the bounds
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
are actions instead of state-action pairs.
This simplifies notation, since the algorithm frequently changes the state associated with an action.

Remark 6.5
.

We implicitly assume that we can continue sampling with an action of our choice:
When we collapse, for example, an EC
(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})
with states
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{R}
into a single representative state, we might enter the EC in state
s
but then continue sampling with an action
a\in{Av}(s^{\prime})
.
This is not an essential restriction:
Upon entering an already detected EC, we can simply pause the algorithm and randomly pick actions in
\mathsf{B}
until we reach the state enabling the next action mandated by the algorithm.

6.3
Proof of Correctness

Now, to prove correctness of the algorithm, we again can reuse a lot of the previous reasoning.
However, we need to invest significant effort in the treatment of end components.
First of all, we again prove that the algorithm is well-defined.

Lemma 6.6
.

During all episodes, we have that
{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\mathbin{\cap}{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s^{\prime})=\emptyset
for all states
s,s^{\prime}\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
with
s\neq s^{\prime}
.

Proof 6.7
.

The algorithm only modifies the set of available actions
{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}
whenever a new representative state
s_{(R,B)}
is added.
In this case, we have
{Av}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s_{(R,B)})\leftarrow C\subseteq\bigcup_{s\in R}{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
and all states of
R
are removed.

Lemma 6.8
.

Algorithm

5
is well-defined.

Proof 6.9
.

To prove this statement, we have to show that (i) no undefined values are accessed, (ii) all assignments are free of contradictions, and (iii) we require no more information than given by
Definition

5.2
.

For (i) and (ii), observe that when assigning the next episode’s variables, we only use the variables of the current episode.
Since we copy all unchanged variables, we only need to take care of the newly introduced arguments, i.e. the representative states
s_{(R,B)}
.
Such a state is only added in
Algorithm

5
.
In the following lines, we define the state’s actions
{Av}
, which is non-empty and disjoint from other states by
Lemma

6.6
.
As no new actions are added, the action values in
s_{(R,B)}
still are defined.
Observe that in
Algorithm

5
the successor oracle is only given states of the original MDP.
Claim (iii) follows immediately.

Now, we show several statements related to the newly added handling of end components.
Our goal is to show that the algorithm essentially samples from a collapsed MDP where the ECs identified by the algorithm are collapsed.
Then, we replicate the proof ideas of the EC-free DQL algorithm on this collapsed MDP in order to again obtain the correctness.

Lemma 6.10
.

Algorithm

5
enters
Algorithm

5
at most
\lvert Act\rvert
times.

Proof 6.11
.

First, observe that due to the pigeon-hole principle,
B
never is empty:
By (
12
), our choice of
\mathsf{i}
is larger than
\lvert Act\rvert
, thus a path of length at least
\mathsf{i}^{2}
contains at least one action
\mathsf{i}
times.
Consequently, whenever the algorithm enters
Algorithm

5
,
B
is non-empty.
Initially, the size of
B
is bounded by
\sum_{s\in S_{1}}\lvert{Av}_{1}(s)\rvert=\lvert Act\rvert
.
We show that in any of the three cases, we remove at least one action which can never occur again as part of
B
.
Consequently, after at most
\lvert Act\rvert
visits to
Algorithm

5
,
B
would necessarily be empty, contradicting the above.

Whenever a state is added to either
T_{\mathsf{e}}
or
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
, this state and its actions will not be considered again—in particular, it will not occur as part of
B
.
For the third case, we show that the number of available actions
\sum_{s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}}\lvert{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\rvert
is reduced whenever a new representative state is added.
In that case, we have
C\leftarrow\bigcup_{s\in R}{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\setminus B
,
S_{\mathsf{e}+1}\leftarrow(S_{\mathsf{e}}\setminus R)\mathbin{\cup}\{s_{(R,B)}\}
, and
{Av}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s_{(R,B)})\leftarrow C
.
By construction of the algorithm and definition of
Appear
, we have
\emptyset\neq B\subseteq\bigcup_{s\in R}{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
.
Using
Lemma

6.6
we thus have
\lvert C\rvert<\lvert\bigcup_{s\in R}{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\rvert
.
Consequently,
\sum_{s\in S_{\mathsf{e}+1}}\lvert{Av}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s)\rvert<\sum_{s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}}\lvert{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\rvert
.

Lemma 6.12
.

Algorithm

5
terminates or experiences an infinite number of episodes.

Proof 6.13
.

Since the length of each episode is limited, i.e. the loop of
Algorithm

5
always terminates after a bounded number of steps, we only need to show that all other loops terminate.
All for-loops iterate over (sub-)sets of states or actions, which are finite by assumption.
The only remaining loop is the computation of
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}
in
Algorithm

5
, where the representative state is resolved.
Observe that by construction of the algorithm, we either have that
\mathsf{collapsed}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)=s
or
\mathsf{collapsed}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)=s_{(R,B)}
with
s\in R
.
Since we only modify
\mathsf{collapsed}
when a new representative state is added, this happens only finitely often, due to
Lemma

6.10
.

Lemma 6.14
.

If we add a representative state
s_{(R,B)}
in
Algorithm

5
after an episode
\mathsf{e}
the bounds of any action
a\in B
are not changed after episode
\mathsf{e}
.

Proof 6.15
.

During each episode
\mathsf{e}
, we only consider states in
S_{\mathsf{e}}
and actions which are available in such states, as the call to
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}
in
Algorithm

5
always yields an element of the current state set
S_{\mathsf{e}}
due to
Lemma

6.16
.
Since all states corresponding to actions in
B
are removed when adding a representative state
s_{(R,B)}
and these actions are not enabled in the newly added state, they do not appear again.

Lemma 6.16
.

For any execution of the algorithm, we always have that
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
for any state
s\in S
.

Proof 6.17
.

We prove by induction:
Initially, we have
\mathsf{rep}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{collapsed}_{1}(s)=s
for all
s\in S_{1}
by definition.
Whenever we modify
S_{\mathsf{e}}
, i.e. remove some states
R
and add a representative
s_{(R,B)}
, we set
\mathsf{collapsed}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s)\leftarrow s_{(R,B)}\in S_{\mathsf{e}+1}
for all
s\in R
.

In order to properly reason about the paths sampled by the algorithm, we introduce a special MDP which corresponds to the current
“view”
of the given MDP.

Definition 6.18
.

For any episode
\mathsf{e}
, let the
sampling MDP

\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}=(S_{\mathsf{e}},Act_{\mathsf{e}},{Av}_{\mathsf{e}},\Delta_{\mathsf{e}})
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\{s\mapsto 1\}\quad\text{for $s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cap}(T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})$, $a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)$, and}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\sum}_{\{s^{\prime\prime}\in S\mid\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s^{\prime\prime})=s^{\prime}\}}\Delta(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}),a,s^{\prime\prime})\quad\text{for other states $s$, $a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)$},

and
Act_{\mathsf{e}}=\bigcup_{s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}}{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
.

Note that the sampling MDP is well-defined due to
Lemmas

6.6
and
6.16
.

Lemma 6.19
.

Fix an execution of the algorithm until some episode
\mathsf{e}
and let
\varrho
be the finite path sampled by the algorithm during episode
\mathsf{e}
.
The probability of sampling this path equals the probability of obtaining this path on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
following the strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
starting in state
\hat{s}
.

Proof 6.20
.

We prove by induction over the path
\varrho
, using the Markov property.
We show that for any finite prefix, the probability of selecting action
a
and then reaching state
s^{\prime}
in the next step is equal in both the algorithm and the sampling MDP.
Observe that we always have
\hat{s}\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
due to
Algorithm

5
and the induction start is trivial.

For the induction step, suppose we are in a state
s
.
By construction of the algorithm,
s\notin T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
The algorithm now uniformly selects an action
a
from
\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
, i.e. with probability
\lvert\mathsf{MaxA}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\rvert^{-1}
for any such action.
Then, a successor
s^{\prime\prime}\in S
is sampled according to
\mathsf{succ}(s,a)
, i.e. with probability
\Delta(s,a,s^{\prime\prime})
.
The overall successor then equals
s^{\prime}=\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s^{\prime\prime})
.
We have
s^{\prime}\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
by
Lemma

6.16
.
Hence, a state
s^{\prime}\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
is sampled with probability
\sum_{\{s^{\prime\prime}\in S\mid\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s^{\prime\prime})=s^{\prime}\}}\Delta(s,a,s^{\prime\prime})
, just as in the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
under strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
.

Assumption 9
.

Whenever the algorithm reaches
Algorithm

5
,
(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})
is an EC of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.

Lemma 6.21
.

The probability that
9
is violated during the execution of
Algorithm

5
is bounded by
\frac{\delta}{4}
.

Proof 6.22
.

We apply
Lemma

6.2
with
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
T=T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
and
\pi=\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
.
By construction of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
and the choice of
T
, we have that
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
trivially satisfies the condition of this lemma, since each state in
T
only has self-loops in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Clearly, we have that
\lvert S_{\pi}\rvert\leq\sum_{s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}}\lvert{Av}(s)\rvert\leq\lvert Act\rvert
, since no actions are added during the execution of the algorithm.
Consequently, we have that either
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond^{\leq 2i^{3}}(T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})]=1
or

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}\!\left[{App}_{\mathsf{i}}\mid\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2\mathsf{i}^{3}}(T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})}\right]\!\geq 1-2(1+\mathsf{i}^{2})\cdot e^{-(\mathsf{i}-1)\frac{p_{\min}(\pi)^{\lvert S\rvert+1}}{\lvert S\rvert+1}}\cdot p_{\min}(\pi)^{-(\lvert S\rvert+1)},

where
{App}_{\mathsf{i}}
are all paths
\rho\in\mathsf{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}}
such that
Appear(\rho,\mathsf{i},2\mathsf{i}^{3})
is an EC in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.

Now, observe that the algorithm only enters
Algorithm

5
if after
2\mathsf{i}^{3}
steps neither
T_{\mathsf{e}}
nor
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
is reached.
By applying
Lemma

6.19
, we get that the probability of
(R,B)
being an EC given that
Algorithm

5
is entered exactly equals
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[{App}_{\mathsf{i}}\mid\overline{\Diamond^{\leq 2\mathsf{i}^{3}}(T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})}]
.
Since
Algorithm

5
is entered at most
\lvert Act\rvert
times due to
Lemma

6.10
, the statement follows by inserting the definition of
\mathsf{i}
from (
12
).

Lemma 6.23
.

Assume that
9
holds and fix some episode
\mathsf{e}
.
Let
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
some state of the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
and
s^{\prime}\in S
such that
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s^{\prime})=s

Then,
s
and
s^{\prime}
have the same value:

\val_{\mathsf{e}}(s)=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\max}[\Diamond T_{\mathsf{e}}]=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s^{\prime}}^{\max}[\Diamond T]=\val(s^{\prime})

Proof 6.24
.

We prove by induction over the episode number.
Initially, we have that
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is quite similar to the original MDP
\mathcal{M}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{Z}_{1}=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{rep}_{1}(s)=s
for all states.
Hence, the only difference lies in the transition function of all states
s\in T
.
These only have self-loops in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, while in
\mathcal{M}
they may have arbitrary transitions.
This is irrelevant for the value of the states, since it equals 1 in both cases.

Now fix an arbitrary episode
\mathsf{e}
.
We have that
\val_{\mathsf{e}}(s)=\val(s^{\prime})

(IH)
for any two states
s
,
s^{\prime}
as in the claim.

\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
is only modified when
Algorithm

5
is entered.
Let
(R,B)
the identified set of states and actions.
Due to
9
,
(R,B)
is an EC of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
To conclude, we distinguish the three cases in the algorithm:

•

T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cap}R\neq\emptyset
: Since
(R,B)
is an EC, any state
s\in R
can reach
T_{\mathsf{e}}
with probability one.
Hence
\val_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s)=1=\val_{\mathsf{e}}(s)=\val(s^{\prime})

6.24
.
In particular, by adding all states of
R
to
T_{\mathsf{e}+1}
, we do not change their value.

•

C=\emptyset
: Once in
R
, this EC cannot be left, i.e.
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\max}[\Diamond\overline{R}]=0
for all
s\in R
.
Consequently, we have that
\val_{\mathsf{e}}(s)=0=\val(s^{\prime})

6.24
.
This value is unchanged by adding the states of
R
to
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}+1}
and thus introducing a self-loop in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.

•

Add a representative state:
By assumption, we have that
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s^{\prime})\in R
and thus
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s^{\prime})=s_{(R,B)}
.
We need to prove that
\val_{\mathsf{e}+1}(s_{(R,B)})=\val(s^{\prime})
.
As
(R,B)
is an EC by assumption, each state in
R
has the same value by
Lemma

4.2
.
The representative state
s_{(R,B)}
has this value by applying the same reasoning as in
Lemma

4.19
.

Lemma 6.25
.

Assume that
9
holds and fix some episode
\mathsf{e}
.
For any EC
(R,B)\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}})
and
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{e}
there exists an EC
(R^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}})
with
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
.

Proof 6.26
.

Note that we do not necessarily have that
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
, since some states of the EC may have been replaced by a representative state.

We prove by induction on the episode
\mathsf{e}
.
Fix any such episode
\mathsf{e}
and EC
(R,B)\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}+1})
.
We only modify the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
when the algorithm enters
Algorithm

5
, hence w.l.o.g. we assume that this happened in episode
\mathsf{e}
.
Let
(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})
be the set of states and actions identified in
Algorithm

5
during episode
\mathsf{e}
.
By
9
,
(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})
is an EC of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
As above, we distinguish the three cases in the algorithm:

•

T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{R}\neq\emptyset
:
Then, all actions in
\mathsf{B}
are changed to a self-loop in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}+1}
and hence we either have
B=\{a\}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
or
B\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{B}=\emptyset
.
In the former case,
(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})
satisfies the conditions of the claim.
In the latter, the EC
(R,B)
already existed in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
, since no state or action of
(R,B)
was modified.

•

C=\emptyset
:
Analogously to the above, all actions in
\mathsf{B}
are now a self-loop in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}+1}
and the same reasoning applies.

•

Add a representative state:
If
s_{(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})}\notin R
, we necessarily have that
\mathsf{B}\mathbin{\cap}B=\emptyset
.
Hence, the EC
(R,B)
again already existed in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
, since none of its components was modified by this step.
If instead
s_{(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{B})}\in R
, we have that
(\mathsf{R}\mathbin{\cup}R,\mathsf{B}\mathbin{\cup}B)
is an EC in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
, following the same reasoning as in
Lemma

4.13
.

Lemma 6.27
.

Assume that
9
holds and fix some step
\mathsf{t}
with corresponding episode
\mathsf{e}
.
Let
(R,B)\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}})
be any EC in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
For any
a\in B
we have that (i) if
\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}})\in\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
, then
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)=0
and (ii)
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)=1
otherwise.

Proof 6.28
.

Item (i) immediately follows from the definition of the algorithm and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
When a state is added to
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
, we set
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)=0
for all its actions.
We prove Item (ii) by induction, showing that the statement holds for all ECs at each step
\mathsf{t}
.
Initially, we have
\mathsf{Up}_{1}(a)=1
for all actions by definition of the algorithm.
For the induction step fix some step
\mathsf{t}
.
We have that
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}(a)=1
for all actions
a
in all ECs without zero-states for all
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{t}

(IH)
.
Now, let
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
be the episode of step
\mathsf{t}+1
and fix any EC
(R,B)
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}
with
R\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}=\emptyset
.
By repeatedly applying
Lemma

6.25
, there exists an EC
(R_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}},B_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}})\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}})
with
B\subseteq B_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}
for all
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{e}
.
Since we have no zero-states in the EC in step
\mathsf{t}+1
, none of the
R_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}
contain zero-states either, by construction of the algorithm and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Thus, the induction hypothesis
6.28
is applicable and we have that
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}(a)=1
for any action
a\in B_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}
and
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{t}
.
Hence, we necessarily have that
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}(s)=1
for all
s\in R_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}
and
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{t}
(also using
Lemma

6.14
).
Whenever any action
a\in B
is selected at any step
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{t}
during episode
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{e}
, all of its successors are part of the EC
(R_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}},B_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}})
, thus
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}(s)=1
for all successors by the above reasoning.
Consequently, we always add a value of
1
to
\texttt{acc}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}(a)
and whenever an
\mathsf{Up}
-update is attempted for action
a
at some step
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{t}
, we would set
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}(a)=1
.

Lemma 6.29
.

Assume that
9
holds and fix some step
\mathsf{t}
with corresponding episode
\mathsf{e}
.
Let
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\geq\mathsf{t}
with episode
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}\geq\mathsf{e}
.
We have for any state
s\in S
that
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}(\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}(s))\leq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s))
and
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s))\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}(\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}(s))
.

Proof 6.30
.

The bounds of actions are modified by (i) the usual update, which only increases or decreases, respectively (ii) in
Algorithms

5
and
5
, where upper bounds are set to
0
and lower bounds set to
1
, or (iii) when an EC is collapsed and thus the set of available actions is modified in
Algorithm

5
.
Cases (i) and (ii) preserve monotonicity of the state bound by definition.
Case (iii) is proven separately for upper and lower bounds, with the proof of the lower bound being significantly more involved.
For the upper bounds, observe that
{Av}_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}(s)\subseteq{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
by definition, i.e. we never add new actions to any state.
Consequently, the maximum over the set of available actions does not increase.
For the lower bounds, we have to show that while collapsing ECs and thus removing actions, we never remove all those which are optimal w.r.t. the lower bound, i.e. all actions
a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
with
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)=\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
.

We proceed by additionally proving an auxiliary statement by induction on the step
\mathsf{t}
in parallel.
In particular, we prove that for any step
\mathsf{t}
with corresponding episode
\mathsf{e}
(i) the statement of the lemma holds
(IH1)
and (ii)
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)\leq\max_{s\in R,a^{\prime}\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\setminus B}\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(a^{\prime})
for all actions
a\in B
(or
0
if no such actions
a^{\prime}
exist) in all ECs
(R,B)\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}})
without a target state, i.e.
R\mathbin{\cap}T_{\mathsf{e}}=\emptyset
.
(IH2)
.

Initially, we have
\mathsf{Lo}_{1}(a)=0
by definition of the algorithm and both statements trivially hold.
For the induction step fix some time step
\mathsf{t}
.
We first treat the case when the lower bound of action an action
a
is successfully updated in step
\mathsf{t}
and later on deal with the case of an EC being collapsed.
Note that
6.30
trivially holds in this case, since the value of
a
is never decreased.
We only need to show the second statement
6.30
, thus assume that the updated action
a
is an internal action of some EC
(R,B)
, i.e.
a\in B
.
For readability, denote
C=\bigcup_{s\in R}{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)\setminus B
the set of outgoing actions of
(R,B)
.
If
C=\emptyset
, the statement follows directly:
Since all lower bounds are initialised to zero, the EC does not contain any target states by assumption, and there are no outgoing actions, the algorithm never updates the lower bound of any action in
B
to a non-zero value.
Thus, assume that
C\neq\emptyset
.
By applying
6.30
to all states of the EC
(R,B)
, we get that
{\max}_{a^{\prime}\in C}\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(a^{\prime})={\max}_{s\in R}\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)

[Fact I]
.
Furthermore, let
k_{1}<\ldots<k_{\overline{m}}=\mathsf{t}
the steps of the most recent visits to
a
with corresponding episodes
\mathsf{e}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\mathsf{e}_{\overline{m}}=\mathsf{e}
and sampled successors
s_{k_{i}}^{\prime}
.
Now, let
R_{i}=\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}(\mathsf{states}_{\mathsf{e}}(R))
for
1\leq i\leq{\overline{m}}
the set of states in episode
\mathsf{e}_{i}
which eventually are collapsed to
R
.
By applying the reasoning of
Lemma

6.25
and
4.13
, there exists a set of actions
B_{i}
with
B\subseteq B_{i}
such that
(R_{i},B_{i})
is an EC in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}
and thus
s_{k_{i}}^{\prime}\in R_{i}

[Fact II]
, since
a\in B_{i}
.
By construction, we have that
\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(R_{i})=R

[Fact III]
.
Finally, we observe that the value of the outgoing actions does not decrease, hence the value we assign to
a
in step
\mathsf{t}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(a)+{\overline{\varepsilon}}\overset{\text{def}}{~=~}\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{i}}(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\ref{proof:dql:lower_bounds_monotone:succ_in_Ri}}{~\leq~}\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}{\max}_{s\in R_{i}}\mathsf{Lo}_{k_{i}}(s)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\ref{proof:dql:lower_bounds_monotone:induction_lemma}}{~\leq~}\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}{\max}_{s\in R_{i}}\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}}(s))

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\ref{proof:dql:lower_bounds_monotone:rep_is_R}}{~=~}\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}{\sum}_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}{\max}_{s\in R_{{\overline{m}}}}\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)

\displaystyle\qquad~=~{\max}_{s\in R_{{\overline{m}}}}\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\ref{proof:dql:lower_bounds_monotone:applied_hypothesis}}{~=~}{\max}_{a^{\prime}\in C_{{\overline{m}}}}\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(a^{\prime}).

This concludes proof of the first part.

For the second part, i.e. when a set of states is collapsed by the algorithm, we have that the collapsed set
(R,B)
is an EC by
9
and
B
are only internal actions.
If the collapsed EC contains target states, the statement trivially holds.
Otherwise, we apply the result of the first part and get that the lower bound assigned to any action in
B
is less or equal to outgoing actions.
Thus, removing the actions in
B
from the set of available actions does not reduce the value of the obtained representative state.

With basic properties about the sampling MDP in place, we can now mimic the previous idea of defining
“converged”
state-action pairs and, using those, show that the algorithm eventually converges with high probability.

Definition 6.31
.

For every step
\mathsf{t}
during episode
\mathsf{e}
, define
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}\subseteq Act_{\mathsf{e}}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{a\mid\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)-\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}),a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}]\rangle\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\}\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{a\mid\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}),a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}]\rangle-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\}.

Again, an action
a
is
\mathsf{Up}
-converged (
\mathsf{Lo}
-converged) at step
\mathsf{t}
if
a\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
(
a\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}
).

Assumption 10
.

Suppose an
\mathsf{Up}
-update of the action
a
is attempted at step
\mathsf{t}
.
Let
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{\overline{m}}=\mathsf{t}
be the steps of the
{\overline{m}}
most recent visits to
a
, and
e_{1}\leq e_{2}\leq\ldots\leq e_{\overline{m}}
the respective episodes.
Then
\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\val_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})\geq\val_{\mathsf{e}_{\overline{m}}}(a)-{\overline{\varepsilon}}
.
Analogously, for an attempted
\mathsf{Lo}
-update, we have
\frac{1}{{\overline{m}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\val_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime})\leq\val_{\mathsf{e}_{\overline{m}}}(a)+{\overline{\varepsilon}}
.

Assumption 11
.

Suppose an update of the upper bound (lower bound) of the action
a
is attempted at step
\mathsf{t}
.
Let
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{\overline{m}}=\mathsf{t}
be the steps of the
{\overline{m}}
most recent visits to
a
.
If
a
is not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged (
\mathsf{Lo}
-converged) at step
k_{1}
, the update at step
\mathsf{t}
is successful.

We replicate most of the statements from the previous DQL algorithm.

Lemma 6.32
.

The following properties hold for
Algorithm

5
.

1.

The number of successful updates of
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
is bounded by
\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
each.

2.

The number of attempted updates of
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
is bounded by
{\overline{\xi}}
.

3.

Assume that
9
holds.
Then, the probability that
10
is violated during the execution of
Algorithm

5
is bounded by
\frac{\delta}{4}
.

4.

Assume that
9
and
10
hold.
Then, we have
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)\leq\val_{\mathsf{e}}(a)\leq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)
for all episodes
\mathsf{e}
, steps
\mathsf{t}\geq\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{e}}
, and actions
a\in Act_{\mathsf{e}}
.

5.

We have for every step
\mathsf{t}
in episode
\mathsf{e}
and state
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
that

\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand \hskip 10.22217pt}\pi_{\mathsf{t}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}](s)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{t}}(s).

6.

If
a\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
, then
a\notin\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{Up}}
for all
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}\geq\mathsf{t}
until an
\mathsf{Up}
-update of action
a
succeeds or the upper bound is set to
0
in
Algorithm

5
.

7.

The probability that
11
is violated during the execution of
Algorithm

4
is bounded by
\frac{\delta}{4}
.

8.

Assume that
11
holds.
If an attempted
\mathsf{Up}
-update of action
a
at step
\mathsf{t}
fails and
\texttt{learn}^{\mathsf{Up}}_{\mathsf{t}+1}(a)=\mathsf{false}
, then
a\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}+1}^{\mathsf{Up}}
.
Once no more updates of
\mathsf{Up}
succeed, the analogous statement holds true for the lower bounds.

9.

Assume that
11
holds.
Then, there are at most
2{\overline{m}}\cdot\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
visits to state-action pairs which are not
\mathsf{Up}
-converged.
Once the upper bounds are not updated any more, there are at most
2{\overline{m}}\cdot\frac{\lvert Act\rvert}{{\overline{\varepsilon}}}
visits to state-action pairs which are not
\mathsf{Lo}
-converged.

Proof 6.33
.

Items

1
and
2
follow directly as in
Lemmas

5.3
and
5.5
.
The only additional observation is that the algorithm never adds new actions and that the changes to the bounds outside of
Algorithm

5
never reset the progress of an action’s bounds.

Item

3
can be proven completely analogous to
Lemma

5.7
, since this proof only relies on the Markov property of the successor sampling.
We need to adjust the definition of
Y_{i}
slightly to incorporate the modifications of the algorithm.
Let thus
s^{\prime}_{k_{i}}\in S
denote the states obtained by the successor oracle in
Algorithm

5
.
By
Lemma

6.23
we have that
\val_{\mathsf{e}}(\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}(s^{\prime}_{k_{i}}))=\val_{\mathsf{e}}(s^{\prime\prime}_{k_{i}})
, and thus
Y_{i}=\val_{\mathsf{e}}(\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}(s^{\prime}_{k_{i}}))
are i.i.d.

For
Item

4
, we first show that all newly introduced updates of
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
are correct.
Using
9
, we prove the two special cases.
The algorithm sets
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{t}}(a)\leftarrow 0
if an EC
(R,B)
without outgoing transitions and no target state is identified.
In this case, we clearly have that
\val_{\mathsf{e}}(a)=0
for all
s\in R
.
Similarly, setting
\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(a)\leftarrow 1
when any state in the EC
(R,B)
is an accepting state is correct, since clearly
\val_{\mathsf{e}}(a)=1
for all
s\in R
,
a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cap}B
.
Due to
Lemma

6.23
, copying the respective bounds to the representative state
s_{(R,B)}
(which happens implicitly in
Algorithm

5
) is correct, too.
Now, the reasoning of
Lemma

5.9
applies.

Items

5
and
6
can be proven as in
Lemma

5.12
.

Item

7
is proven analogous to
Item

3
, following the proof of
Lemma

5.14
.
Again, this claim only depends on the sampled successors.
We define
X_{i}=\pi_{k_{1}}[\mathsf{Up}_{k_{1}}](\mathsf{rep}_{\mathsf{e}_{1}}(s_{k_{i}}^{\prime\prime}))
.
Since we do not modify the underlying transition probabilities, from which
s_{k_{i}}^{\prime\prime}
is obtained, these
X_{i}
are i.i.d. again and we can apply the same reasoning.
To conclude the proof as before, we need to employ
Lemma

6.29
.
Note that since we only speak about the actual computed bounds
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
, we do not need to employ
Lemma

6.23
.

Item

8
follows directly as in
Lemma

5.16
.
Similarly,
Item

9
follows as in
Lemma

5.18
, using
Item

1
instead of
Lemma

5.3
.

In the proof of correctness for the no-EC DQL algorithm, we applied
Lemma

A.9
directly on the MDP to obtain bounds on the reachability of
s_{+}
based on the values of
\mathsf{Up}
and
\mathsf{Lo}
in
Lemma

5.20
.
Now, we cannot apply this lemma directly on either
\mathcal{M}
or
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
since both may contain ECs.
Hence, we apply the lemma on an MDP derived from
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
to obtain a similar result.
Let us thus first define the set of all actions in
“non-final”
ECs as

E_{\mathsf{e}}={\bigcup}_{\{(R,B)\in\EcsOp(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}})\mid R\mathbin{\cap}(T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})=\emptyset\}}B.

Lemma 6.34
.

Assume that
9
and
10
hold and fix an episode
\mathsf{e}
.
Then, we have for every state
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}

\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond E_{\mathsf{e}}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond T_{\mathsf{e}}].

Proof 6.35
.

We first want to derive an MDP from
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
without any ECs but still capturing its behaviour.
For this, recall that there are two kinds of ECs in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Firstly, there are ECs which correspond to ECs in the original
\mathcal{M}
.
Secondly, we get a self-loop EC for each identified target- or zero-state, i.e. states in
T_{\mathsf{e}}
or
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
We define the derived MDP
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}=(S_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\{s_{+},s_{-}\},Act_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\{a_{+},a_{-}\},\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime},{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime})
, where

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s_{\circ},a_{\circ})

\displaystyle=\{s_{\circ}\mapsto 1\}\quad\text{for $\circ\in\{+,-\}$}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\{s_{+}\mapsto 1\}\quad\text{for all $s\in T_{\mathsf{e}}$, $a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)$,}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\{s_{-}\mapsto 1\}\quad\text{for all $s\in\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}$, $a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)$,}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\{s_{+}\mapsto 1\}\quad\text{for all $a\in E$, $s=\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}})$,}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\quad\text{for all other $s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}$, $a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)$},

and
{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s)={Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
for
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
and
{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s_{\circ})=\{a_{\circ}\}
for
\circ\in\{+,-\}
.
In essence,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
equals
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
except that we (i) added the special states
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
, (ii) all states in
T_{\mathsf{e}}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
move to
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
, respectively, and (iii) all actions in ECs outside of
T_{\mathsf{e}}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
move to
s_{+}
, in the spirit of
Lemma

6.27
.

Clearly,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
has no ECs except the special states
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
and thus satisfies
1
.
Moreover, the probability of reaching
s_{+}
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
equals the probability of reaching
T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}E_{\mathsf{e}}
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
by construction of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}

[Fact I]
.

Now, we extend
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
to select action
a_{\circ}
in the special state
s_{\circ}
to obtain
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Furthermore, we set
X(s,a)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(a)
for all states
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
,
a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
,
X(s_{+},a_{+})=1
, and
X(s_{-},a_{-})=0
.
We apply
Lemma

A.9
with
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
\pi=\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
\kappa_{l}=-1
, and
\kappa_{u}=3{\overline{\varepsilon}}
.
As a result, for each state
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
we have

\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert},

where
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is the MDP defined in the lemma.
Observe that for
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}

[Fact II]

\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)={\sum}_{a\in{Av}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)}\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\cdot X(s,a)={\sum}_{a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)}\pi_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\cdot\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(a)=\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}](s).

To analyse how
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
are related, we first need to derive the structure of
\mathcal{K}
from the lemma.
Thus, we now prove that
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}\mathbin{\cup}\{a_{+},a_{-}\}
.
Recall that

\mathcal{K}=\{a\in Act_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\{a_{+},a_{-}\}\mid X(s,a)-\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)\langle\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X]\rangle\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\}

and

\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)\langle\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X]\rangle={\sum}_{s^{\prime}\in S_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\{s_{+},s_{-}\}}\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a,s^{\prime})\cdot{\sum}_{a^{\prime}\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime})}\pi(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\cdot X(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}).

Clearly,
a_{+}
and
a_{-}
satisfy the requirements due to their self-loop.
Furthermore, we have
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s_{+})=1
,
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s_{-})=0

[Fact III]
.
Now, let
a\in Act_{\mathsf{e}}
and
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
the corresponding state.
By definition, we have
X(s,a)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(a)
, hence we need to show that
\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)\langle\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X]\rangle=\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}]\rangle
.
We proceed with a case distinction.

•

s\in T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
: By definition of the algorithm, we have
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)=1
or
0
, respectively.
The unique successor under any action
a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
equals
s
by definition, thus
\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}]\rangle=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
.
In
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
, the unique successor equals
s_{+}
or
s_{-}
, respectively.
Thus, with
6.35
, we have
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)=\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}](s)
.
The claim follows.

•

a\in E
: Note that this case implies that
s\notin T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Due to
Lemma

6.27
, we have that
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(a)=1
for all such actions.
Recall that
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}
follows actions maximizing
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Consequently,
\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}](s^{\prime})=\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s^{\prime})=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s^{\prime})=1
for all states
s^{\prime}
inside an non-trivial EC of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Thus, we also have
\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}]\rangle=1
.
From the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
and
6.35
, we directly get
\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)\langle\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X]\rangle=1
.

•

s\notin T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
a\notin E
: By definition, we have
\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)=\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)
.
Together with
6.35
and
6.35
, the statement follows.

Recall that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is defined as
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
except that
\Delta^{\prime}(s,a)=\{s_{+}\mapsto X(s,a),s_{-}\mapsto 1-X(s,a)\}
for all
a\notin\mathcal{K}
.
Hence, as in
Lemma

5.20
, we get that for all states
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}],

and thus with
6.35
we get
[Fact IV]

\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond(T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}E_{\mathsf{e}})].

Further, we have
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)=\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}](s)=\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
by
Lemma

6.32
,
Item

5

[Fact V]
.

To conclude the proof, we show that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]

[Fact VI]
.
To this end, observe that (i) for each state
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
and action
a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
we either have
\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)=\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)
or
\support\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\subseteq\{s_{+},s_{-}\}
and (ii) the added states
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
are absorbing.
Thus, each run reaching
\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
has a corresponding, equally probable path in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.

The overall claim follows from the above equations and a union bound.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql:upper_bound:strategy_correspondence}}}{~=~}\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql:upper_bound:K_reach_correspondence}}}{~\leq~}\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\mathclap{\ref{proof:dql:upper_bound:probability_bound}}}{~\leq~}\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond(T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}E_{\mathsf{e}})].

Lemma 6.36
.

Assume that
9
and
10
hold and fix an episode
\mathsf{e}
.
Then, we have for every state
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond T_{\mathsf{e}}]\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)+3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}]+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond E_{\mathsf{e}}].

Proof 6.37
.

As in
Lemma

6.34
, we construct a second MDP without ECs, but slightly modify the transition function.
In particular, let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}=(S_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\{s_{+},s_{-}\},Act_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\{a_{+},a_{-}\},\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime},{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime})
be defined as before.
However, for
a\in E_{\mathsf{e}}
and
s=\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}})
, we define

\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}(s,a)=\{s_{+}\mapsto\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}]\rangle,s_{-}\mapsto 1-\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}]\rangle\}.

Again,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
has no ECs except in the two special states and thus
Lemma

A.9
is applicable.
We set
X(s,a)=\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(a)
for all states
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
,
X(s_{+},a_{+})=1
, and
X(s_{-},a_{-})=0
.
As above, we have that
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)=\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}](s)
for all
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
.
We apply the lemma with
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
\pi=\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
\kappa_{l}=-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}
, and
\kappa_{u}=1
.
Thus, for each state
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]-\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)\leq 3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert},

where
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is the MDP defined in the lemma.
We again show that
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}\mathbin{\cup}\{a_{+},a_{-}\}
by case distinction as follows:

•

Trivially,
a_{+},a_{-}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s_{+})=1
, and
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s_{-})=0
.

•

s\in T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
: The claims follow by an analogous argument.
Recall that for these states we have
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(a)=\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(a)
for all
a\in{Av}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
.

•

a\in E
: Inserting the definitions, we get

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X]\rangle=\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a,s_{+})\cdot\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s_{+})+\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a,s_{-})\cdot\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s_{-})

\displaystyle\qquad=\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}]\rangle\cdot 1+(1-\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}]\rangle)\cdot 0

\displaystyle\qquad=\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)\langle\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}]\rangle.

•

s\notin T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
a\notin E
: Follows analogously.

As in
Lemma

5.20
, we also get for all states
s\in S_{\mathsf{e}}
that

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}].

Similar to the above proof, we have
\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}[X](s)=\pi_{\mathsf{e}}[\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}](s)\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)
by
Lemma

6.32
,
Item

5
.
With completely analogous reasoning, we can show that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}]
.
Putting all equations together, we get that

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(s)+3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}].

Now, it remains to show that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond T_{\mathsf{e}}]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond E_{\mathsf{e}}]\leq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}},s}^{\pi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]
.
This claim follows with the same reasoning as before, since we have that
\Delta_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)=\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{e}}(s,a)
for
a\notin E_{\mathsf{e}},s=\mathop{\mathsf{state}}(a,\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}})
.
Thus, every path in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
which does not visit
E
has a corresponding, equally probable path in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}
.
The overall claim follows.

Theorem 6.38
.

Algorithm

5
terminates and yields a correct result with probability at least
1-\delta
after at most
\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{POLY}(\lvert Act\rvert,p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert},\varepsilon^{-1},\ln\delta))
steps.

Proof 6.39
.

This proof is largely analogous to the proof of
Theorem

5.22
, and we shorten some of its parts.
Again, we only consider executions where
11
,
10
and
9
hold.
By
Lemmas

6.21
and
6.32
,
Items

7
and
3
together with the union bound, this happens with probability at least
1-\delta
.
Correctness of the result upon termination follows from
Lemma

6.32
,
Item

4
.

We show by contradiction that the algorithm terminates for almost all considered executions.
Thus, assume that the execution does not halt with non-zero probability.
By
Lemma

6.12
, all of these executions experience an infinite number of episodes.

Due to
Lemma

6.32
,
Item

2
, there are only finitely many attempted updates on all considered executions and the algorithm eventually does not change
\mathsf{Up}
, since no successful updates can occur from some step
\mathsf{t}
onwards.
Similarly, there are only finitely many EC collapses due to
Lemma

6.10
, and eventually the sampling MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
stabilizes.
This means that all following samples are obtained by sampling according to the strategy
\pi_{\mathsf{t}}
on the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Again, we employ
Lemma

A.13
to continue the proof and we get
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]=0
and
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}]=0
on almost all considered executions.
By an analogous argument, we can show that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{t}}}[\Diamond E_{\mathsf{e}}]=0
, since otherwise by
Lemma

6.2
(with
T=T_{\mathsf{e}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}
) we have a non-zero probability of detecting a new EC, contradicting our assumption.

Thus, by applying
Lemma

6.34

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond T_{\mathsf{e}}]\geq\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s})-3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Up}}}]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond E_{\mathsf{e}}]>\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s})-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Dually, with
Lemma

6.36
we get
