\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond T_{\mathsf{e}}]\leq\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s})+3{\overline{\varepsilon}}\cdot\lvert S\rvert p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{Lo}}}]+\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{e}},\hat{s}}^{\pi_{\mathsf{e}}}[\Diamond E_{\mathsf{e}}]<\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Together,
\mathsf{Up}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s})-\mathsf{Lo}_{\mathsf{e}}(\hat{s})<\varepsilon
, contradicting the assumption.

For the step bound, we can mostly replicate the idea of the DQL variant without ECs.
In particular, we can bound the number of paths by the same argument:
The probability of reaching a non-
\mathsf{Up}
- / non-
\mathsf{Lo}
-converged action within
\lvert S\rvert
steps is at least
p_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
(or 0).
By
Lemma

6.32
,
Item

9
we again get that the number of visits to such actions is bounded.
Since
\mathsf{i}\geq\lvert Act\rvert\geq\lvert S\rvert
and thus the sampling is not stopped early due to that condition, we again can bound the maximal number of paths by the same
n
.
For the length of the paths, observe that they are bounded by
2\mathsf{i}^{3}
by construction of the algorithm.
From the definition of
\mathsf{i}
in Equation (
12
), we see that this bound is polynomial, too, by considering the Taylor expansion of the exponential.

Remark 6.40
.

To conclude, we briefly outline extensions to other objectives.

For safety, i.e. maximizing the probability of remaining inside a given set of states forever (or, equivalently, minimizing reachability of unsafe states), we only need to change the treatment of end components slightly.
Assume w.l.o.g. that any unsafe state is collapsed into one sink state
s_{-}
(e.g. by testing for every encountered state whether it is safe and, if not, replace it by
s_{-}
).
Then, whenever we identify an end component, we know that this end component does not contain a sink state but rather only comprises safe states.
However, this actually is exactly what we are looking for: a possibility of staying safe forever.
Thus, we assign a value of
1
to all actions in such an EC.
And indeed, by
Lemma

2.9
, we know that ECs are the
only
place that allow us to stay safe forever.
Together, we can derive the desired result.

Extending to total reward has two major hurdles.
Firstly, the total reward can be infinite, and we would first need to identify whether this is the case.
To this end, we need to identify
all
end components in the system and check for each that it yields zero reward.
Here, we would need to employ graph-based reasoning akin to
[
10
]
, as we need to ensure that we have not missed any transition.
Once this is established (or guaranteed due to domain knowledge), we can derive an upper bound on the total reward if we are given an upper bound on the reward that can be obtained in one step
r_{\max}
.
This bound is in the order
\mathcal{O}(p_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}\cdot r_{\max})
.
Using this bound as initial value for the upper bound then would lead to a correct algorithm.
(See also
[
115
, Appendix B]
and
[
51
, Section 4]
for related discussions.)

Finally, an extension to mean payoff (aka. long run average reward) or general
\omega
-regular objectives in a model-free setting seems to be rather unlikely.
Both inherently are infinite horizon objectives, while sampling only ever gives us finite information.
As such, we likely need to use graph-based reasoning to reach meaningful conclusions.
In particular, for
\omega
-regular objectives, we would need to know at least the graph structure of identified end components to decide whether they are winning or not, and for mean payoff we even would need bounds on the transition probabilities.
As a special case, models where each end component is guaranteed to only comprise a single state could be tractable.

7
Conclusion and Future Work

In this work, we improved and extended the ideas of
[
33
]
, fixing several imprecisions and issues of the proofs.
This results in a framework for verifying MDP, using learning algorithms.
Building upon exiting methods, we thus provide novel techniques to analyse infinite-horizon reachability properties of arbitrary MDPs, yielding either exact bounds in the white-box scenario or probabilistically correct bounds in the black-box scenario.
Moreover, we presented a generalization of the methods of
[
33
]
, allowing for further, more sophisticated applications.

We deliberately omit an experimental evaluation.
Since the inception of the presented idea, multiple tools have implemented variants and extensions thereof for several objectives and model classes.
In particular, we want to point to the tool PET
[
113
,
115
]
, which implements and evaluates the general complete information algorithm and presents a detailed evaluation.
Moreover, as already mentioned, for DQL the associated constants are infeasible for practical application:
Already for an MDP with
10
States,
20
actions and
p_{\min}=0.1
, we obtain
{\overline{m}}\approx 10^{26}
for
\varepsilon=0.1
and
\delta=0.01
.

Given this framework, an interesting direction for future work would be to extend this approach with more sophisticated learning algorithms.
Another, orthogonal direction is to explore whether our approach can be combined with symbolic methods.

Appendix A
Auxiliary Statements

In this chapter we provide some general statements about Markov chains and decision processes which are used in various proofs for the DQL algorithms.

From Reachability to Step-bounded Reachability

In this section we prove several statements relating the infinite-horizon reachability with the reachability after a sufficiently large number of steps.

Lemma A.1
.

For any Markov chain
\mathsf{M}=(S,\delta)
, state
s
, and target set
T
, we have that either
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond T]=0
or
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond^{\leq\lvert S\rvert}T]\geq\delta_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
, where
\delta_{\min}
is the minimal transition probability, i.e.
\delta_{\min}=\min\{\delta(s,s^{\prime})\mid s\in S,s^{\prime}\in\support\delta(s)\}
.

Proof A.2
.

Fix the Markov chain
\mathsf{M}
, state
s
, and target set
T
as in the lemma.
In the first case there is nothing to prove, thus assume that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond T]>0
.
This means that there exists a finite path
\varrho
from
s
to some state in
T
.
By the pigeon-hole principle, we can assume this path has length at most
\lvert S\rvert
.
Clearly, the probability of any single transition on this path is at least
\delta_{\min}
and thus the overall probability of this path is at least
\delta_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert}
.

Corollary A.3
.

For any MDP
\mathcal{M}=(S,Act,{Av},\Delta)
, memoryless strategy
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{MD}}
, state
s
, and target set
T
, we have that either
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond T]=0
or
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond^{\leq\lvert S\rvert}T]\geq\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\lvert S\rvert}
, where
\delta_{\min}(\pi)=\min\{\pi(s,a)\cdot\Delta(s,a,s^{\prime})\mid s\in S,a\in{Av}(s),\pi(s,a)>0,s^{\prime}\in\support\Delta(s,a,s^{\prime})\}
.

Proof A.4
.

Follows directly from the above lemma by applying it to
\mathcal{M}^{\pi}
.

The following lemma shows that on a large enough horizon, step-bounded and unbounded reachability values coincide up to a small error, similar in spirit to
[
94
, Lemma 2]
.

Lemma A.5
.

Given a Markov chain
\mathsf{M}=(S,\delta)
, a state
s\in S
, a constant
\tau\in(0,1]
, and a target set
T
, for
N\geq\ln(\frac{2}{\tau})\cdot\lvert S\rvert\delta_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}
we have

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond T]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond^{\leq N}T]\leq\tau.

Proof A.6
.

We can express
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond T]
as a sum of
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond^{\leq N}T]
and
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond^{>N}T]
, where
\Diamond^{>N}T=\Diamond T\setminus\Diamond^{\leq N}T
are all paths which reach the set
T
but only after at least
N+1
steps.
Clearly,

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond T]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond^{\leq N}T]=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond^{>N}T].

By
[
34
, Lemma 5.1]
we have that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathsf{M},s}[\Diamond^{>N}T]\leq 2\cdot c^{N}
, where
c=\exp(-\lvert S\rvert^{-1}\delta_{\min}^{\lvert S\rvert})
.

\displaystyle 2\cdot c^{N}\leq\tau\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad N\cdot\ln c\geq\ln\frac{\tau}{2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad N\geq\ln\frac{\tau}{2}\cdot(\ln c)^{-1}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad N\geq\ln\frac{\tau}{2}\cdot-\lvert S\rvert\delta_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad N\geq\ln\frac{2}{\tau}\cdot\lvert S\rvert\delta_{\min}^{-\lvert S\rvert}

Unique Solution of Bellman Equations

Now, we prove that a particular class of Bellman equations has a unique solution by proving that the associated functor is a contraction.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP,
{Av}_{?}:S\to Act
a function mapping a state
s
to a subset of its available actions
{Av}_{?}(s)\subseteq{Av}(s)
,
c:S\to\mathbb{R}
a cost function, and
\pi
a memoryless strategy on
\mathcal{M}
.
Define
S_{=}=\{s\mid{Av}_{?}(s)=\emptyset\}
.

If
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond S_{=}]>0
for all states
s\in S
, then the system of Bellman equations

f(s)=c(s)+{\sum}_{a\in{Av}_{?}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot\Delta(s,a)\langle f\rangle

has a unique solution
f
.

Proof A.8
.

Define the iteration operator
F
as

\displaystyle F(f)(s)=c(s)+{\sum}_{a\in{Av}_{?}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot\Delta(s,a)\langle f\rangle.

Trivially, a function
f:S\to\mathbb{R}
is a solution to the equation system if and only if it is a fixed point of
F
, i.e.
F(f)(s)=f(s)
for all states
s\in S
.

We show that
F^{\lvert S\rvert}
, i.e.
F
applied
\lvert S\rvert
times, is a contraction and thus has a unique fixed point, obtainable by iterating
F
.
This means that there exists a contraction factor
0\leq\gamma<1
such that for two arbitrary
f,g:S\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\max_{s\in S}\left\lvert F^{\lvert S\rvert}(f)(s)-F^{\lvert S\rvert}(g)(s)\right\rvert\leq\gamma\cdot\max_{s\in S}\left\lvert f(s)-g(s)\right\rvert.

(13)

Let
P(s,s^{\prime},k)
be the probability of reaching state
s^{\prime}
starting from
s
in exactly
k
steps using the strategy
\pi
by using only actions from
{Av}_{?}
.
Note that for
s\in S_{=}
this implies
P(s,s^{\prime},k)=0
for any
s^{\prime}\in S
and any number
k
.
For
s\in S_{?}\coloneqq S\setminus S_{=}
, we have that

F^{\lvert S\rvert}(f)(s)={\sum}_{s^{\prime}\in S}\left({\sum}_{i=0}^{\lvert S\rvert-1}P(s,s^{\prime},i)\cdot c(s^{\prime})\right)+{\sum}_{s^{\prime}\in S_{?}}P(s,s^{\prime},\lvert S\rvert)\cdot f(s^{\prime})

Observe that the first term is independent of
f
, hence for
s\in S_{?}
we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert F^{\lvert S\rvert}(f)(s)-F^{\lvert S\rvert}(g)(s)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\lvert{\sum}_{s^{\prime}\in S_{?}}P(s,s^{\prime},\lvert S\rvert)\cdot f(s^{\prime})-{\sum}_{s^{\prime}\in S_{?}}P(s,s^{\prime},\lvert S\rvert)\cdot g(s^{\prime})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\leq{\sum}_{s^{\prime}\in S_{?}}P(s,s^{\prime},\lvert S\rvert)\cdot\left\lvert f(s^{\prime})-g(s^{\prime})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left({\sum}_{s^{\prime}\in S_{?}}P(s,s^{\prime},\lvert S\rvert)\right)\cdot\max_{s^{\prime}\in S}\left\lvert f(s^{\prime})-g(s^{\prime})\right\rvert.

By assumption, we have that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond S_{=}]>0
.
This implies that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond^{\leq\lvert S\rvert}S_{=}]\geq\delta_{\min}(\pi)>0
by
Corollary

A.3
.
For
s\in S_{=}
, observe that
F^{\lvert S\rvert}(f)(s)=f(s)=c(s)
and hence

\left\lvert F^{\lvert S\rvert}(f)(s)-F^{\lvert S\rvert}(g)(s)\right\rvert=\left\lvert f(s)-g(s)\right\rvert=\left\lvert c(s)-c(s)\right\rvert=0.

Consequently,
\gamma=\max_{s\in S_{?}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{?}}P(s,s^{\prime},\lvert S\rvert)\leq\delta_{\min}(\pi)<1
satisfies Inequality (
13
) and we have that
F^{\lvert S\rvert}
is a contraction.
By the Banach fixed point theorem we get that
F^{\lvert S\rvert}
has a unique fixed point and thus the equation system has a unique solution.

From Local to Global Error Bounds

The next lemma bounds the overall error of an approximation in an MDP given that the approximation is
“close”
locally.
By definition

\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi[X]\rangle={\sum}_{s^{\prime}\in S}\Delta(s,a,s^{\prime})\cdot{\sum}_{a^{\prime}\in{Av}(s^{\prime})}\pi(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\cdot f(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}).

Thus, the term
X(s,a)-\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi[X]\rangle
in the lemma essentially denotes the difference between the state-action value
X(s,a)
and the expected value obtained from
X
in the successors of
(s,a)
following
\pi
.
Consequently,
\mathcal{K}
contains those state-action pairs for which the value under
X
is consistent with the value of its successors up to some error.

Lemma A.9
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,Act,{Av},\Delta)
be an MDP satisfying
1
,
X:S\times{Av}\to[0,1]
a function assigning a value between
0
and
1
to each state-action pair,
\pi
a memoryless strategy on
\mathcal{M}
, and
\kappa_{l}\leq\kappa_{u}
two error bounds.
Set

\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\{(s,a)\mid\kappa_{l}\leq X(s,a)-\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi[X]\rangle\leq\kappa_{u}\}.

Define a new MDP
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=(S,Act,{Av},\Delta^{\prime})
where

\Delta^{\prime}(s,a)=\begin{cases}\Delta(s,a)&if $(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}$, and\\
\{s_{+}\mapsto X(s,a),s_{-}\mapsto 1-X(s,a)\}&otherwise.\end{cases}

Then, for each state
s\in S
we have

\kappa_{l}\leq\frac{\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\lvert S\rvert}}{\lvert S\rvert}\left(\pi[X](s)-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]\right)\leq\kappa_{u},

where
\delta_{\min}(\pi)=\min\{\pi(s,a)\cdot\Delta(s,a,s^{\prime})\mid s\in S,a\in{Av}(s),\pi(s,a)>0,s^{\prime}\in\support(\Delta(s,a))\}
is the smallest transition probability in the Markov chain
\mathcal{M}^{\pi}
.

Proof A.10
.

Define
v^{\prime}(s)=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond\{s_{+}\}]
.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{K}(s)=\{a\in{Av}(s)\mid(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}\}
and
\lnot\mathcal{K}(s)=\overline{\mathcal{K}(s)}\mathbin{\cap}{Av}(s)
the sets of all actions
a\in{Av}(s)
such that
(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}
and
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{K}
, respectively.
Observe that
v^{\prime}
is a solution to the following system of equations:

\displaystyle v^{\prime}(s_{+})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle v^{\prime}(s_{-})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle v^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle={\sum}_{a\in\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot\Delta(s,a)\langle v^{\prime}\rangle+{\sum}_{a\in\lnot\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot X(s,a)

We apply
Lemma

A.7
to show that
v^{\prime}
is the unique solution.
Let
\varepsilon(s_{+})=1
,
\varepsilon(s_{-})=0
, and
\varepsilon(s)={\sum}_{a\in\lnot\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot X(s,a)
for all other
s\in S
.
Further, set
{Av}_{?}(s_{+})={Av}_{?}(s_{-})=\emptyset
and
{Av}_{?}(s)=\mathcal{K}(s)
for all other
s\in S
.
Then,
\{s_{+},s_{-}\}\subseteq S_{=}
.
The MDP
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
also satisfies
1
, since no new ECs are introduced, and thus
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond S_{=}]=1>0
for all
s\in S
by
Lemma

2.9
.
Consequently,
Lemma

A.7
is applicable and
v^{\prime}
is the unique solution of the above equations.

\pi[X]
satisfies a similar set of equations:

\displaystyle\pi[X](s_{+})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\pi[X](s_{-})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\pi[X](s)

\displaystyle={\sum}_{a\in{Av}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot X(s,a)

\displaystyle={\sum}_{a\in\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot X(s,a)+{\sum}_{a\in\lnot\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot X(s,a)

\displaystyle=\kappa(s)+{\sum}_{a\in\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi[X]\rangle+{\sum}_{a\in\lnot\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot X(s,a)

where
\kappa(s)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot(X(s,a)-\Delta(s,a)\langle\pi[X]\rangle)
is bounded by
\kappa_{l}\leq\kappa(s)\leq\kappa_{u}
.
Again, by
Lemma

A.7
, these equations then have a unique fixed point, setting
\varepsilon(s)=\kappa(s)+{\sum}_{a\in\lnot\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot X(s,a)
.

Now, we prove a bound for the difference between
X
and
v^{\prime}
using the above characterizations.
Observe that the above equation systems only differ structurally by the error term
\kappa(s)
.
Let thus
f(s)=\pi[X](s)-v^{\prime}(s)
.
This
f
is a fixed point of the following equation system:

\displaystyle f(s_{+})

\displaystyle=f(s_{-})=0

\displaystyle f(s)

\displaystyle=\kappa(s)+{\sum}_{a\in\mathcal{K}(s)}\pi(s,a)\cdot\Delta(s,a)\langle f\rangle

Clearly,
f
again is unique by
Lemma

A.7
.

Given a state
s
, the probability to reach the terminal states
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
in
\lvert S\rvert
steps following strategy
\pi
is bounded from below by
\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\lvert S\rvert}
due to
Corollary

A.3
.
Consequently, the probability of not reaching these states in
\lvert S\rvert
steps is bounded from above by
1-\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\lvert S\rvert}<1
.
Hence, we can bound the difference between
\pi[X]
and
v^{\prime}
by

\kappa(s)\cdot{\sum}_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert S\rvert\left(1-\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{\lvert S\rvert}\right)^{n}=\kappa(s)\cdot\lvert S\rvert\delta_{\min}(\pi)^{-\lvert S\rvert}.

Bounding Reachability on Similar MDP

In this lemma, we show that MDP which are sufficiently
“similar”
also have similar reachability values.
In particular, we are concerned with MDP that agree on a subset of states.
For another notion of similarity (same transition structure but different transition probabilities) see
[
44
]
.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,Act,{Av},\Delta)
be an MDP,
T\subseteq S
a set of target states,
\mathcal{K}\subseteq S\times{Av}
a set of state-action pairs, and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},Act^{\prime},{Av}^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
an arbitrary MDP with
\mathcal{K}\subseteq S^{\prime}\times{Av}^{\prime}
that coincides with
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{K}
and
T
, i.e. (i)
{Av}(s)={Av}^{\prime}(s)
for all
s\in\mathcal{K}
, (ii)
\Delta(s,a)=\Delta^{\prime}(s,a)
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{K}
, and (iii)
T\subseteq S^{\prime}
.
Moreover, let
\pi
be a strategy in
\mathcal{M}
,
s\in S\mathbin{\cap}S^{\prime}
an arbitrary state in both MDP, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
a natural number.
Then,

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond^{\leq N}T]\geq\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}}[\Diamond^{\leq N}T]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond^{\leq N}\overline{\mathcal{K}}],

where
\pi^{\prime}
is an arbitrary strategy equal to
\pi
on all finite paths over
\mathcal{K}
, i.e.
\pi(\varrho)=\pi^{\prime}(\varrho)
for all
\varrho\in\mathcal{K}^{\star}\times S\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{FPaths}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof A.12
.

For a finite path
\varrho=s_{1}a_{1}\ldots a_{n-1}s_{n}\in\mathsf{FPaths}_{\mathcal{M}}
, let
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\varrho]
denote the probability of path
\varrho
occurring when following strategy
\pi
from state
s
.
Let
\mathcal{K}_{N}
denote the (finite) set of all finite paths
\varrho
of length
N
starting in
s
such that all state-action pairs
(s_{i},a_{i})
in
\varrho
are in
\mathcal{K}
.
Similarly, let
\lnot\mathcal{K}_{N}
denote the set of all such paths containing at least one state-action pair not in
\mathcal{K}
.
Let
\mathcal{R}(\varrho)
be a function which returns
1
if some target state of
T
is in path
\varrho
and
0
otherwise.
Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}}[\Diamond^{\leq N}T]-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond^{\leq N}T]

(14)

\displaystyle\qquad=\begin{aligned} \sum_{\mathclap{\varrho\in\mathcal{K}_{N}}}\left(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}}[\varrho]\cdot\mathcal{R}(\varrho)-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\varrho]\cdot\mathcal{R}(\varrho)\right)+&\\
\qquad\sum_{\mathclap{\varrho\in\lnot\mathcal{K}_{N}}}\left(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}}[\varrho]\cdot\mathcal{R}(\varrho)-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\varrho]\cdot\mathcal{R}(\varrho)\right)&\end{aligned}

(15)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\varrho\in\lnot\mathcal{K}_{N}}\left(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}}[\varrho]\cdot\mathcal{R}(\varrho)-\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\varrho]\cdot\mathcal{R}(\varrho)\right)

(16)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{\varrho\in\lnot\mathcal{K}_{N}}\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}}[\varrho]\cdot\mathcal{R}(\varrho)

(17)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{\varrho\in\lnot\mathcal{K}_{N}}\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}}[\varrho]

(18)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}[\Diamond^{\leq N}\overline{\mathcal{K}}]

(19)

In Equation (
15
), we simply split the set of all paths of length
N
into
\mathcal{K}_{N}
and
\lnot\mathcal{K}_{N}
.
For Equations (
16
) and (
19
), note that
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s}^{\pi^{\prime}}
and
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},s}^{\pi}
agree on
\mathcal{K}_{N}
by choice of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime}
.

Repeating Events in Markov Processes

Finally, we prove a general statement of Markov processes.
The statement itself seems to be quite obvious, yet surprisingly tricky to prove.
In essence, we want to show the following.
Suppose that we are given a Markov process
X_{t}
on some probability space
\Omega
together with a sequence of events
A_{t}
.
Moreover, assume that for a significant set of atoms
\omega\in\Omega
there is an infinite set of times
T
such that the
conditional
probability of
A_{t}
occurring is at least
\varepsilon>0
, i.e.
\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\in A_{t}\mid X_{t-1}(\omega)]>\varepsilon
.
Then, the set of atoms for which infinitely many
A_{t}
actually occur is also significant.
The subtle difficulty of this statement arises from the fact that (i) conditional probabilities are considered, and (ii) the set
T
depends on the particular atom
\omega
.

Lemma A.13
.

Fix some probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and a measure space
(S,\mathcal{S})
.
Let
X_{t}:\Omega\to S
be a Markov process on
\Omega
and
A_{t}\in\mathcal{S}
measurable events in
S
.
Assume that the set
\Omega^{\prime}=\{\omega\in\Omega\mid\exists T.~\lvert T\rvert=\infty\land\forall t\in T.~\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\in A_{t}\mid X_{t-1}](\omega)>\varepsilon\}
has positive measure, i.e.
\mathbb{P}[\Omega^{\prime}]>0
, and that
\Omega^{\prime}_{t}=\{\omega\in\Omega\mid\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\in A_{t}\mid X_{t-1}](\omega)>\varepsilon\}
is measurable for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,
\mathbb{P}[\{\omega\in\Omega\mid\exists T.~\lvert T\rvert=\infty\land\forall t\in T.~X_{\mathsf{t}}(\omega)\in A_{t}\}]=\mathbb{P}[\Omega^{\prime}]
.

Proof A.14
.

Let
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
.
By assumption, for each such
\omega
, there exists an infinite set of time-points
\mathsf{Tries}(\omega)=\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots\}
with
1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots
where
\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\in A_{t}\mid X_{t-1}](\omega)>\varepsilon
.
We call such an event a
try
of
\omega
.
Denote
\mathsf{Try}_{i}(\omega)=t_{i}
or
\infty
if no such
t_{i}
exists, e.g. for
\omega\notin\Omega^{\prime}
.
Informally,
\mathsf{Try}_{i}
is the time of the
i
-th try of some outcome
\omega
.

\mathsf{Try}_{i}
is measurable by assumption, since its pre-images can be constructed using
\Omega^{\prime}_{t}
.
Moreover, let
\mathsf{Succs}(\omega)=\{s_{1},s_{2},\cdots\}\subseteq\mathsf{Tries}(\omega)
be the times where
X_{s_{j}}(\omega)\in A_{s_{j}}
, called
j
-th success(ful try)
.
Note that
\mathsf{Succs}(\omega)
possibly is finite or even empty for some outcomes
\omega
, even for
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
, since infinitely many tries may fail.
Now, let
\mathsf{Succ}_{j}(\omega)=s_{j}\in\mathsf{Succs}(\omega)
the time of the
j
-th success or
\infty
if no such
s_{j}
exists, i.e.
j>\lvert\mathsf{Succs}(\omega)\rvert
.

\mathsf{Succ}_{j}
is measurable since
\mathsf{Try}_{i}
,
X_{t}
and
A_{t}
are measurable.
To succinctly capture corner-cases, we further define
\mathsf{Succ}_{0}=0
.
The successes
\mathsf{Succs}(\omega)
naturally partition the set
\mathsf{Tries}(\omega)
into
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{j}(\omega)=\{t\in\mathsf{Tries}(\omega)\mid\mathsf{Succ}_{j}(\omega)<t\leq\mathsf{Succ}_{j+1}(\omega)\}
.
We use
\mathsf{TryJ}_{i,j}(\omega)
to refer to the
i
-th element of
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{j}(\omega)
, or
\infty
if no such element exists.

\mathsf{TryJ}_{i,j}
is measurable due to
\mathsf{Succ}_{j}
being measurable.
Informally,
\mathsf{TryJ}_{i,j}(\omega)
denotes the time of the
i
-th try since the
j
-th success.

We show that after a sufficient number of tries, there is a success with high probability.
Repeating this argument inductively, we then show that there are infinitely many successes for almost all outcomes
\omega
in
\Omega^{\prime}
.

Let thus
\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}
denote the set of runs which at time
t
have succeeded
j
times and since the
j
-th success experienced
i
-th tries, where this
i
-th try happens exactly at time
t
.
Formally,

\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}\coloneqq\{\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{TryJ}_{i,j}(\omega)=t\}.

Note that this definition implicitly includes the condition
\mathsf{Succ}_{j}(\omega)\leq t<\mathsf{Succ}_{j+1}(\omega)
by definition of
\mathsf{TryJ}_{i,j}
.
Thus,
\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}
are disjoint for fixed
i
and
j
.

We furthermore define
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}=\bigcup_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}=\{\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{TryJ}_{i,j}(\omega)<\infty\}
as the set of outcomes which after their
j
-th success experienced at least
i-1
11
11

11

\mathsf{TryJ}_{i,j}(\omega)=t
does not exclude that the try at time t is successful.
unsuccessful tries.
We have
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}=\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i+1,j}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}
, since the
i
-th try either fails and the
i+1
-th try is experienced later (since
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
, implying infinitely many tries) or the try succeeds.
Observe that
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i+1,j}
and
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}
are not disjoint, since, for example, the runs succeeding at the
i+1
-th try also are an element of
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}
.
On the contrary, we show that
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}\setminus\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}]=0
, i.e. almost all runs in
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}
will eventually succeed again.

To this end, we argue that for any fixed
j
we have that
\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}]=0
.
Fix some
j
and
i
with
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}]>0
(otherwise there is nothing to prove, since
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}
is monotonically decreasing in
i
).
Let
\mathsf{TryTimesJ}_{i,j}=\{t\mid\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]>0\}
which is non-empty by the previous condition.
Clearly,
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}]=\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]=\sum_{t\in\mathsf{TryTimesJ}_{i,j}}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]
, as
\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}
are disjoint.
Observe that
\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}
is the intersection of several conditions on
X_{t^{\prime}}
for
t^{\prime}<t
and requiring that
\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\in A_{t}\mid X_{t-1}]>\varepsilon
.
Hence, by the Markov property

\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\notin A_{t}\mid\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]=1-\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\in A_{t}\mid\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]=1-\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\in A_{t}\mid X_{t-1}]<1-\varepsilon.

Intuitively, this means that the probability of a try at time
t
succeeding does not depend on the number of previous tries and successes.
Thus, for all
t\in\mathsf{TryTimesJ}_{i,j}
, we have
\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\notin A_{t}\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]<(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]
.
Observe that
\bigcup_{t=1}^{\infty}(X_{t}\notin A_{t}\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j})=\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i+1,j}
since the intersection implies that the
i
-th try at time
t
was unsuccessful.
Together,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i+1,j}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[{\bigcup}_{t=1}^{\infty}X_{t}\notin A_{t}\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]={\sum}_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\notin A_{t}\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]

\displaystyle={\sum}_{t\in\mathsf{TryTimesJ}_{i,j}}\mathbb{P}[X_{t}\notin A_{t}\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]

\displaystyle<{\sum}_{t=1}^{\infty}(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\mathbb{P}[{\bigcup}_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{TryAtTJ}^{t}_{i,j}]

\displaystyle=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}].

Consequently,
\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}]=0
for any fixed
j
.

As argued before, we have
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}=\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i+1,j}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}
.
Iterating this equation yields
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}=\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i+k,j}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}
for any
k\geq 1
and consequently
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}\mathbin{\cup}\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}
.
Informally, this equation can be read as
“all outcomes which succeed at least
j
times either try infinitely often or succeed at least
j+1
times.”

Let
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{\infty,j}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}=\{\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{Succ}_{j}(\omega)<\infty=\mathsf{Succ}_{j+1}(\omega)\}
.
Clearly,
\mathsf{TriesJ}_{\infty,j}\mathbin{\cap}\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}=\emptyset
, thus we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}\setminus\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{\infty,j}]
.
Additionally, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{\infty,j}]=\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{i,j}]=0
by the above reasoning.
Hence
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j+1}\setminus\mathsf{TriesJ}_{1,j}]=0
.
This implies that almost all runs in
\Omega^{\prime}
succeed infinitely often, concluding the proof.
